# Miracles and extraordinary events attributed to Thérèse of Lisieux: merged list

Stage 2b merge of the four `parts/*/miracles.md` files (M-EW, M-LF, M-OM, M-OW; 390 source entries). Each entry is a report, not a finding. The Church approved only four miracles of Thérèse. Every other entry keeps its own honest status label. Statuses and medical facts are copied from the source entries. Entries that only look alike are kept apart and cross-referenced.

Numbers M-001, M-002 and so on are new merged numbers. The source IDs (M-EW-, M-LF-, M-OM-, M-OW-) are listed in each entry. Cards (F-...) are cited inside the copied text and are never renumbered. Entries are ordered by date inside each group.

## Counts

| Group | Merged entries |
|---|---|
| A. Approved by the Church (four miracles) | 4 |
| B1. Investigated by the Church: canonical inquiry or tribunal examined the case | 5 |
| B2. Investigated by the Church: presented to the process as a case (vice-postulator's Articles 1910 or Mère Agnès's 1915 summary), not approved | 69 |
| C. During her life (1873 to her death, 30 September 1897) | 51 |
| D. After her death (sworn testimony, Carmel records and popular reports) | 166 |
| E. Appendix 1: family and earlier events that are not attributed to Thérèse | 13 |
| F. Appendix 2: collective and summary reports, and predictions (not single events) | 14 |
| Total merged entries | 322 |


## Merge summary

- Source entries read: 390 (M-EW 204, M-LF 45, M-OM 103, M-OW 38).
- Merged entries that combine two or more source entries: 48. They absorb 79 duplicate source entries.
- Merged entries built from part of one source entry (52 entries use a source entry in part; the source entry then appears in more than one merged entry because it holds several events).
- Entries flagged "no extraordinary claim or no outcome": 7. They stay in the list because the source part listed them.
- Only four healings were approved: Charles Anne (1906) and Louise de Saint-Germain (1916) for the beatification in 1923; Maria Pellemans (1923) and Gabriella Trimusi (1923) for the canonization in 1925. The two miracles of Pietro Schilirò (2002) and Carmen Pérez Pons (2008) belong to the cause of Louis and Zélie Martin and are in Appendix 1.
- The sections "What was reported", "The person helped", "Witnesses", "Status in the sources", "Medical or natural facts (as in the sources)" and "Doubts and other versions (as in the sources)" copy the source entries. A source ID in square brackets, such as [EW-044], marks which source entry a passage comes from. The text under "What was reported" of a merged entry is a plain-language merge of the sources, and "Where the sources differ" lists the disagreements.
- Status labels: "approved", "investigated by canonical inquiry", "listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910)", "listed in Mère Agnès's 1915 summary of 54 dossiers", "sworn at the Church process", "recorded by the Carmel or shrine", "family report or tradition", "her own report", "popular report" and "personal report or belief of the witness". A label is a description of who reported the event. It is not a finding that the event was a miracle.

## Open conflicts and points for later stages

1. The smile of the Virgin (13 May 1883): the date is 10 May for Céline and Pauline; Thérèse says the Virgin "seemed" beautiful while Pauline and Laveille say the statue moved; Thérèse says the cure came "gently", the sisters say instantly.
2. Charles Anne: Laveille and Taylor say an instant cure; the Carmel and the process record show a course over days (fever normal from 10 Sept 1906). The certifying doctor was related to the Martin family.
3. Louise de Saint-Germain: cure on 22 Sep (Laveille, Carmel) or 25 Sep 1916 (Taylor); the start of her illness and the date of the last sacraments differ by source.
4. Maria Pellemans: "March 1923" (bull) or "24 March" (Taylor). Gabriella Trimusi: the bull gives no day for the cure.
5. Gallipoli: the sum is 500 francs, lire or livres; the prioress "dreamed" or was awake; several witnesses are second-hand (Giannattasio).
6. Mrs Dorans: 27 Aug 1909 (right) or 1910 (Taylor 1926/30); "carcinoma of the sigmoid flexure" or "neoplasm"; the process edition omits her deposition.
7. Debossu (place and year), Weber (1909 or 1910; name garbled by Taylor), Derrien (1904 immediate cure or 1911 gradual), Augouard (1909 or 1910), Aubry (cancer or gangrenous ulcer; petal date), the kettle (23 Feb 1910 or "about Lent 1910"; how full the tank was), Grant (wording of the message; timing of the money from Glasgow).
8. Possible duplicates kept apart or merged with a flag: Luchini and the Piacenza parcel (M-EW-052, M-EW-058); the London "prodigious fact" and Miss Clark (M-EW-051, M-OM-019); Céline's flame of Oct 1897 and the ring of light round the moon (M-EW-190, M-EW-033); Aubry and the unnamed old man with tongue cancer (M-EW-082, M-EW-111).
9. Coverage gaps in the source parts: about 100 of more than 3,200 published accounts of the Pluie de roses are in the list (Taylor, Laveille and the Carmel web selection only); the Guise thesis and the Pluie de roses volumes were not read; the Arras and Paris miracle processes of 1921 (F-OM-041) are not identified; the Édith Piaf eyesight story is not in these sources; no weather record for 10 Jan 1889 was checked.

## A. Approved by the Church (four miracles)

### M-001 — Healing of the seminarian Abbé Charles Anne, Lisieux and Bayeux, 1906
- Date: Sep 1906
- Status label: approved for beatification (decree of 11 Feb 1923)
- What was reported: Charles Anne, a native of Lisieux and seminarian at Bayeux, aged 23, had failing health from June 1904, with haemoptyses in 1905 and 1906. After a haemorrhage on 23 Aug 1906 he was taken to bed with large cavities in the lungs, burning fever and total aversion to food. Death was expected within days. Dr Francis La Néele (24 Aug 1906) found a tubercular lesion of the right upper lung with softening, then the left lung, and the Koch bacillus in the sputum. A relic of Thérèse (a sachet of her hair) hung round his neck. A first novena failed and a violent haemorrhage followed. He refused to accept death and made a second novena asking Thérèse herself. Laveille and Taylor: at a moment of confidence he pressed the relic to his heart and prayed ("Little Thérèse, you are in heaven ... You must cure me"; Taylor: "I did not come here to die! I came to work for God! You must cure me!"). He sat up at once, with the struggle for breath, pain and fever gone, and doctors called in haste found him well (Laveille: "new lungs" had formed, his own gloss). The Carmel account and the process record give a gradual course: improvement "from the next day" (Carmel), fever falling from Monday 3 Sept and normal from Monday 10 Sept, cavity gone, only a scar found by La Néele about 18 Sept, certificate of 7 Mar 1909. On 3–4 Aug 1917, during the Apostolic Process, Drs Leprévost and de Cornière examined him and found excellent health. He became a priest: curate of Pont-l'Évêque (Maupas; Jeanne La Néele), and chaplain of the Lisieux hospital serving 800 patients (Laveille; 1925 in the Laveille PDF, 1928 in M-LF-015). The Church experts agreed it was an organic lesion that nature could not restore in that way; the bull calls it a miracle "of the same nature and earlier" than Sr Louise's.
- The person helped: [OM-001] Charles Anne, native of Lisieux, aged 23 (Carmel; Taylor). Health failed from June 1904; collapse after a haemorrhage on 23 Aug 1906 (Laveille; Carmel: taken to bed 23 Aug 1906); large cavities in the lungs, burning fever, total aversion to food; death expected within days. [LF-015] Abbé Charles Anne, seminarian [EW-044] Abbé Charles Anne [EW-110] Abbé Anne, curate of Pont-l'Évêque [EW-137] Abbé Anne, later curate of Pont-l'Évêque
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-001] the nursing sister, Sœur Marie Saint-Ignace (Clémence François) (Carmel); Dr Paul Loisnel (Carmel); first-person account by Anne dated 29 Jan 1907 (Carmel). Taylor heard the story from Anne himself in Rome in 1923; Anne and the old nun who nursed him attended the beatification. On 3–4 Aug 1917, during the Apostolic Process, Drs Adolphe-Jean Leprévost and Alexandre-Damase de Cornière (the Carmel doctor who treated Thérèse) examined him and found him in excellent health (Carmel). [LF-015] the nursing Sister; the doctors; Laveille, 1925 [EW-044] Dr Francis La Néele; the abbé himself; a second doctor [EW-110] Dr La Néele (as physician) via Jeanne [EW-137] Maupas SAW him dying and saw him well later
- Status in the sources: [OM-001] **approved for beatification** — decree of 11 Feb 1923 [LF-015] approved for beatification (one of the two miracles chosen for the decree of 11 Feb 1923, per Laveille) [EW-044] approved for the 1923 beatification (per plan §8.1 and Laveille; D3 is before approval) [EW-110] hearsay in sworn testimony at the Apostolic Process; this cure was one of those considered for the beatification (not stated in this file) [EW-137] listed in the Articles, no. 126; sworn testimony
- Official diagnosis: [OM-001] « tubercolosi florida, hemoptoica, in periodo cavitaria » — "florid tuberculosis with haemoptysis, in the cavity stage". Three experts appointed by the Congregation agreed it was an organic lesion that nature could not restore in that way (AAS 15 p. 169; AAS 17 p. 341). The bull calls it a miracle "of the same nature and earlier" than Sœur Louise's.
- His words (Laveille PDF 410) [OM-001]: "Little Thérèse, you are in heaven; of that I feel sure. I am on earth where there is good to be done. You must cure me." Taylor (PDF 276): "I did not come here to die! I came to work for God! You must cure me!"
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [OM-001] large pulmonary cavities; tubercle bacillus in sputum; a deep cavity in the right lung and damaged bronchi (Carmel); two doctors judged the case incurable (Carmel). Treatment named: none beyond nursing; prayer, relic. [LF-015] advanced pulmonary consumption, haemorrhage, cavities, fever, no food; no drugs named. [EW-044] pulmonary tuberculosis, bacillus positive, cavity; La Néele was Thérèse's cousin's husband (a family link to note) [EW-137] at the point of death; no diagnosis given here
- Where the sources differ: Speed: Laveille and Taylor say instant; the Carmel says improvement "from the next day" after the second novena; M-EW-044 says the fever fell from 3 Sept and was normal from 10 Sept, and the cavity had gone by about 18 Sept. Novenas: Taylor and the parents (M-EW-044) say the first novena went to Our Lady of Lourdes through Thérèse and the second to her alone "in order that the saving of his priestly vocation might be manifestly due to her". Maupas (M-EW-137) notes that others prayed to Our Lady of Lourdes at the same time. Words of the prayer differ between Laveille and Taylor. Exact day of cure is not given (between late Aug 1906 and 29 Jan 1907; the Carmel dates his own account 29 Jan 1907). The certifying doctor, La Néele, was married to Thérèse's cousin (a family link). The Carmel names the nursing sister as Sr Marie Saint-Ignace (Clémence François) and Dr Paul Loisnel as a witness. M-EW-110 and M-EW-137 (Jeanne La Néele and Maupas) add no medical facts.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [OM-001] Taylor says the first novena was to Our Lady of Lourdes through her intercession, the second to her alone "in order that the saving of his priestly vocation might be manifestly due to her". Exact day of cure not given (between late Aug 1906 and 29 Jan 1907). Later life: priest; chaplain of the public hospital of Lisieux, serving 800 patients (Laveille PDF 410–411, 1925). [LF-015] Other dossier parts hold the process documents. Laveille's "new lungs" is his own gloss. [EW-044] the certifying doctor was related to the Martin family [EW-110] no details in this deposition [EW-137] Others prayed at the same time to Our Lady of Lourdes for him (Maupas notes this himself).
- Source entries: M-OM-001, M-LF-015, M-EW-044, M-EW-110, M-EW-137
- Sources cited: [OM-001] AAS 15 pp. 169–170 (decree); AAS 17 p. 341 (bull); Laveille PDF 409–411 (cites Summarium of 1919 p. 989 § 2844); Taylor 1926/30 PDF 276; Taylor 1924 PDF 21; Carmel "Les miracles de la Béatification". Public-domain (AAS, Laveille, Taylor); unknown (Carmel). [LF-015] LAV p.409–411, p.426 [EW-044] D3 art. 125 pp. 140–143 (PDF 154–157) [EW-110] PA-23 [1360] [EW-137] PO-25, f. 1231v

### M-002 — Healing of Sr Louise de Saint-Germain, Ustaritz (Ustarritz), September 1916
- Date: 22 Sep 1916
- Status label: approved for beatification (decree of 11 Feb 1923)
- What was reported: Sr Louise de Saint-Germain, a teacher of the Daughters of the Cross (provincial house at Ustaritz, Basses-Pyrénées, attached to a Spanish convent), had a bleeding stomach ulcer. She received the last sacraments in 1915 and asked only for a good death. In early September 1916 another sister persuaded her to pray again. On the night of 10 Sep 1916 Thérèse came and said: "Be generous; you will soon be cured; I promise it." In the morning the three sisters who slept in the infirmary found rose petals of different colours scattered round her bed. She grew worse. On the evening of 21 Sep, after a violent attack, she fell asleep, and woke the next morning cured, hungry and asking to go to Mass. The superior sent her back to bed with a big breakfast, and she returned to her duties. Laveille adds that Thérèse had earlier favoured her with heavenly perfumes and that "formal certificates of renowned doctors" attest the cure. The Church experts agreed it was a most grave haemorrhagic ulcer of the stomach.
- The person helped: [OM-002] Sœur Louise de Saint-Germain, Congregation of the Daughters of the Cross, provincial house of Ustaritz (Basses-Pyrénées); a teacher attached to a Spanish convent; ill since her novitiate (1911–12, Taylor) or from 1912 (Laveille) / early 1913 after profession (Carmel). [LF-017] Sr Louise de Saint-Germain
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-002] her own account (Laveille, citing *Journal des Pèlerins*, 5–18 Oct 1924); three infirmary sisters (petals); her superior. [LF-017] Sr Louise (Journal des Pèlerins, 5–18 Oct 1924); three sisters; doctors (unnamed)
- Status in the sources: [OM-002] **approved for beatification** — decree of 11 Feb 1923 [LF-017] approved for beatification (the second miracle chosen for the decree of 11 Feb 1923, per Laveille)
- Official diagnosis: [OM-002] « gravissimo ulcere ventriculi, formae hemorragicae » — "a most grave ulcer of the stomach, haemorrhagic in form"; three experts agreed (AAS 17 p. 341).
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [OM-002] frequent haemorrhages; twice tried to return to teaching and was stopped by haemorrhages (Laveille); crisis on 14 Nov 1913 with 25 days of water only; recurrence 1915 with heavy bleeding, surgery thought necessary; 32 days on iced water only; ulcer running through the pylorus to the upper duodenum (Carmel). Taylor: certificate of her doctor "confirmed by an X-ray photograph and by Dr. le Bec of Paris, the famous surgeon, who wrote a remarkable paper to prove the supernatural character of the cure" (the Carmel page does not name Le Bec). [LF-017] stomach ulcer with haemorrhages; last sacraments; no treatment named.
- Where the sources differ: Date of cure: Laveille and the Carmel say she fell asleep on the evening of 21 Sep and woke cured on 22 Sep 1916; Taylor says she woke cured "on the morning of September 25". Onset of illness: 1911–12 (Taylor), 1912 (Laveille), early 1913 after profession (Carmel). Last sacraments: "in 1915" (Taylor), "at the beginning of 1915" (Laveille), summer 1915 (Carmel). Taylor names a certificate "confirmed by an X-ray photograph and by Dr. le Bec of Paris"; the Carmel page does not name Le Bec. Laveille cites an article of 1924 (Journal des Pèlerins, 5–18 Oct 1924) for an event of 1916; the printed process volumes were not seen. Taylor 1926/30 says a great picture in St Peter's showed the shower of roses at this cure (1925).
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [OM-002] **Date of cure:** Laveille and the Carmel: fell asleep evening of 21 Sep, woke cured 22 Sep 1916. Taylor: woke cured "on the morning of September 25". Taylor also says the last sacraments came "in 1915" and Laveille "at the beginning of 1915"; the Carmel says summer 1915. Taylor 1926/30 PDF 23: "a great picture in St. Peter's" showed the shower of roses at this cure (1925). [LF-017] Laveille cites a 1924 article for an event before the 1923 decree; the printed process volumes were not seen.
- Source entries: M-OM-002, M-LF-017
- Sources cited: [OM-002] AAS 15 pp. 169–170; AAS 17 pp. 340–341; Laveille PDF 425–426; Taylor 1926/30 PDF 276–277; Taylor 1924 PDF 21; Carmel "Les miracles de la Béatification". [LF-017] LAV p.425–426

### M-003 — Healing of Maria (Marie) Pellemans at the tomb, Lisieux, March 1923
- Date: 24 Mar 1923
- Status label: approved for canonization (decree of 19 Mar 1925)
- What was reported: Maria Pellemans, a young Belgian woman, had recognized pulmonary tuberculosis by October 1909. Tubercular enteritis and gastritis followed. She was treated at home and in the sanatorium "La Hulpe". In August 1920 she went on pilgrimage to Lourdes without result. In March 1923 she joined a pilgrimage group visiting the Lisieux Carmel. Praying with confidence on the tomb of Blessed Teresa, she at once recovered perfect health (bull, from her own account). Taylor: she came "in a dying state" a few days before the translation, and "next day, March 24, at the grave ... she was suddenly and completely restored"; the disease had for many years ravaged her. Canon Barette of Brussels preached the details at Lisieux in Sept 1925. Official diagnosis: chronic tuberculosis of the lungs and intestines. Three doctors appointed by the Congregation gave favourable written opinions on both canonization cures.
- The person helped: [OM-004] Maria (Marie) Pellemans, Belgian ("young Belgian girl", Laveille and Taylor) [LF-036] Marie Pellemans
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-004] her own narrative (bull); apostolic process at Mechelen (Malines) validated 11 Jun 1924; Canon Barette of Brussels preached on the cure at Lisieux in Sept 1925 (Laveille PDF 444). [LF-036] Laveille
- Status in the sources: [OM-004] **approved for canonization** — decree of 19 Mar 1925 [LF-036] approved for canonization (one of the two miracles for the canonization, per Laveille)
- Official diagnosis: [OM-004] « a chronica pulmonum atque intestinorum tuberculosi » — chronic tuberculosis of the lungs and intestines. Three doctors appointed by the Congregation gave favourable written opinions on both canonization cures (AAS 17 p. 344).
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [OM-004] sanatorium La Hulpe; prior care at home; Lourdes pilgrimage 1920. [LF-036] tuberculosis
- Where the sources differ: Date: "March 1923" (bull), "March 24" (Taylor). The grave was then still in the cemetery (translation on 26 Mar). Laveille (M-LF-036) gives only "grave tubercular disease" and the Barette sermon.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [OM-004] Date: "March 1923" (bull) and "March 24" (Taylor). The grave was then still in the cemetery (translation on 26 Mar). The plan asked "at her tomb, 1923, to verify": confirmed. [LF-036] Other dossier parts hold the process documents.
- Source entries: M-OM-004, M-LF-036
- Sources cited: [OM-004] AAS 17 pp. 149–150 (decree), 343–344 (bull, image checked); Taylor 1926/30 PDF 279; Laveille PDF 438; Carmel "Historique". [LF-036] LAV p.438, p.444

### M-004 — Healing of Sr Gabriella (Gabrielle) Trimusi, Parma, June 1923
- Date: Jun 1923
- Status label: approved for canonization (decree of 19 Mar 1925)
- What was reported: Sr Gabriella Trimusi joined the Poor Daughters of the Sacred Hearts (mother house at Parma; called "Chieppine" by Taylor and Laveille) at 23. In 1913 her left knee began to hurt (she used to break firewood across it). Two doctors' remedies failed. After three years she was sent to "Medianum" (probably Milan) for heliotherapy, baths, blisters and injections. Four years later her spine was affected. Back at Parma, several doctors judged the lesion tubercular. An X-ray of the knee showed periostitis of the head of the tibia. In the public hospital she was given radiation and caught the "Spanish" influenza, and the back pain grew worse. On 13 Jun 1923, on the advice of the ecclesiastical assistant, a novena began before a small picture with a prayer to Blessed Teresa; she prayed more for other sisters than for herself. The last day of the novena was the last day of the triduum for the beatification at the nearby Carmelite church. She went there with other sisters and came back slowly and painfully. When the superior told her to pray, she knelt on the sick knee without pain. After supper she took off her corset in the first room upstairs and cried "Sanata sum! sanata sum!". She returned to all her work. Official diagnosis: chronic arthrosynovitis of the left knee and chronic spondylitis of the lower dorsal spine, both tubercular ("Koch's bacilli" first in the knee, then the spine).
- The person helped: [OM-003] Sister Gabriella (Gabrielle) Trimusi, Poor Daughters of the Sacred Hearts, Parma [LF-037] Gabrielle Trimusi
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-003] her superior and sisters; apostolic process at Parma validated 11 Jun 1924. [LF-037] Laveille
- Status in the sources: [OM-003] **approved for canonization** — decree of 19 Mar 1925 [LF-037] approved for canonization
- Official diagnosis: [OM-003] « a chronica arthrosinovite sinistri genu, simulque a chronica spondilite infimae spinae dorsualis, utraque naturae tubercularis » — chronic arthrosynovitis of the left knee and chronic spondylitis of the lower dorsal spine, both tubercular; "Koch's bacilli" first in the knee, then the spine (AAS 17 p. 149).
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [OM-003] two doctors, then several; heliotherapy, baths, vesicatories, injections at "Medianum"; general remedies; X-ray; radiation in hospital; Spanish flu; the corset. [LF-037] tuberculosis
- Where the sources differ: The bull does not give the exact day of cure (the novena began 13 Jun 1923; nine days suggests about 21–22 Jun, not stated). Taylor dates the triduum "June, 1923" and says "she felt an irresistible impulse to discard all artificial support". The cause's lawyer argued the case counted as two miracles; the Congregation did not accept this (F-OM-068). Laveille calls it "grave tubercular disease" and spells the congregation "Chieppine". Apostolic process at Parma validated 11 Jun 1924.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [OM-003] The cause's lawyer argued it counted as two miracles; the Congregation did not accept this (F-OM-068). Taylor: "arthritis in the knee, and … tuberculosis of the spine"; at the end of the triduum "she felt an irresistible impulse to discard all artificial support". Taylor dates the triduum "June, 1923". The bull does not give the exact day of the cure (the novena began 13 Jun 1923; nine days suggests about 21–22 Jun; not stated). [LF-037] Same as M-LF-036; the spelling "Chieppine" is Laveille's.
- Source entries: M-OM-003, M-LF-037
- Sources cited: [OM-003] AAS 17 pp. 148–150 (decree; p. 150 image checked); AAS 17 pp. 342–343 (bull); Taylor 1926/30 PDF 279–280; Laveille PDF 438; Carmel "Historique" ("double tubercular disease"). [LF-037] LAV p.438

## B1. Investigated by the Church: canonical inquiry or tribunal examined the case

### M-005 — Mrs Dorans (Hélène Knight, widow Dorans), Glasgow: instant cure of a cancerous tumour, 27 August 1909
- Date: 27 Aug 1909
- Status label: examined by the diocesan tribunal (1911); not an approved miracle; Taylor calls it the best-proven grace
- What was reported: A widowed mother of twelve, of the parish of Our Lady and St Margaret, Glasgow, had a painful tumour of the left abdomen for years (11 years, M-EW-070). It was judged inoperable at the Western Infirmary (April–May 1909) and she was sent home to die; for ten weeks she could keep no food. A Sister of Mercy proposed a novena to "the Little Flower" from Sunday 22 Aug 1909. On the Thursday night she slept for the first time in years. About 5.30 a.m. on Friday 27 Aug she felt a gentle pressure on both shoulders and a warm breath, "knew that an invisible Presence was beside her bed", saw clearly and felt no pain. The tumour was gone except a lump smaller than a marble, which was gone next day. She asked for a roll and a cup of tea. Her Protestant doctor, expecting to find her dying, said "If this is going to be permanent, it is nothing short of a miracle." Her doctor certified the cure on 21 Jan 1910 (M-EW-070); Dr Thomas Colvin certified on 14 Jul 1910; an X-ray by Dr Riddell (c. July 1910) was normal. She was examined for the Lisieux tribunal in Aug 1911 by Drs La Néele and de Cornière (abdomen normal), and re-examined on 17 May 1912 by Drs Rankin, Carmichael and Colvin. Colvin read a paper on the case at Plymouth on 26 May 1912. She testified at the Ordinary Process on 9 Aug 1911 (session 81).
- The person helped: [EW-056] Mrs Dorans of Glasgow (Hélène/Helen Knight, widow Dorans, mother of twelve children) [EW-141] Hélène Knight, widow Dorans, Glasgow [OM-020] Mrs Dorans, parish of Our Lady and St Margaret, Glasgow
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-056] Fr Taylor (PO-02); Mrs Dorans herself testified (PO-28, 9 August 1911, session 81, f. 1261r–1266v of the public copy) [EW-141] herself [OM-020] Dr George Carmichael (her doctor, non-Catholic); Professor Gemmell (Chair of Medicine, Glasgow) diagnosed "Carcinoma of the sigmoid flexure of the colon" and called it "a large, tender, almost immobile tumour"; certificate of Dr Thomas Colvin, 14 Jul 1910 (examined her 21 Jun 1910); X-ray by Dr Riddell (c. July 1910) normal; examined for the Lisieux tribunal in August 1911 by Drs La Néele and de Cornière (abdomen normal); re-examined 17 May 1912 by Drs Alexander Rankin, Carmichael and Colvin; Colvin read a paper on the case at Plymouth, 26 May 1912.
- Status in the sources: [EW-070] in the Articles; she testified at the diocesan tribunal (D6) [EW-056] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process; Taylor calls it the best-proven and most extraordinary grace [EW-141] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process, 9 August 1911, session 81, f. 1261r–1266v; omitted from the printed edition [OM-020] recorded by the Carmel; **examined by the diocesan tribunal (1911)**, but not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-070] "neoplasm" per doctor; residual small lump [EW-056] only "cancerous tumour" and that she was dying; all medical detail is in the omitted folios [OM-020] hard tumour size of an orange in left lumbar region; chronic intestinal catarrh; swelling of legs; blood passed; emaciation; vomiting; operation judged certainly fatal; X-ray afterwards.
- Where the sources differ: Date: Taylor 1926/30 says "August 27, 1910"; Taylor 1924 (with certificates dated 1910) gives 27 Aug 1909, which the dossier treats as correct. Tumour duration: "for years" (M-OM-020) or 11 years (M-EW-070). Diagnosis: Professor Gemmell called it "Carcinoma of the sigmoid flexure of the colon" (M-OM-020); the doctor's certificate says "neoplasm" (M-EW-070); the Ordinary Process edition says only "cancerous tumour" and prints neither Taylor's narrative nor her deposition (omitted folios f. 190r–202v and 1261r–1266v). The editors say she spoke only of her illness and cure.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-056] The edition prints neither Taylor's narrative nor Knight's deposition. The editors say Knight spoke only of her illness. The novena was suggested by a Sister of Mercy of Glasgow (f. 205r). Her cure increased devotion at the Notre Dame convent. [EW-141] The editors omitted it because it did not touch virtues or fame of holiness. [OM-020] Taylor 1926/30 dates the cure "August 27, 1910". Taylor 1924 (detailed, with certificates dated 1910) gives 27 Aug 1909. 1909 is clearly right.
- Source entries: M-EW-070, M-EW-056, M-EW-141, M-OM-020
- Sources cited: [EW-070] D3 art. 142 pp. 180–185 (PDF 194–199); D6 pp. 398–401 (PDF) adds that she was summoned before the ecclesiastical tribunal at Bayeux/Lisieux [EW-056] PO-02, f. 188r (detailed narrative f. 190r–194v and documents f. 194v–202v are omitted in the edition); PO-28 editors' note [EW-141] PO-28 editors' note [OM-020] Taylor 1924 PDF 396–401; Taylor 1926/30 PDF 422

### M-006 — Gallipoli: the 500 francs (lire, livres) left by an apparition, night of 16 January 1910
- Date: 16 Jan 1910
- Status label: investigated by canonical inquiry (Bishop Müller of Gallipoli; reported to Rome); not an approved miracle
- What was reported: The poor Carmel of Gallipoli (Apulia) was in debt (300 francs) and at times skipped dinner. It ended a triduum to the Trinity through Thérèse's intercession on 16 Jan 1910. Near dawn that day the prioress, Mother Maria Carmela of the Heart (Sacred Heart) of Jesus, suffering from the after-effects of pleurisy and awake all night, felt someone touch the bedclothes and a hand cover her. A voice said God uses those in heaven as on earth and brought her 500 francs for the community's debt; told the debt was only 300, the voice said "the rest will be over and above". By "bilocation" she was led through the corridors to the turn room or parlour by a young Carmelite shining with light. There, in a drawer of the accounts desk, a small box held the money (ten 50-franc notes of the Bank of Naples) and the unpaid bill. The visitor said: "No, I am not our holy Mother! I am the Servant of God, Sœur Thérèse of Lisieux. To-day, in heaven and on earth, we keep the feast of the Holy Name of Jesus", laid a hand on the prioress's head and arranged her veil. When the prioress offered to guide her out, she answered: "No, no, my way is sure, and I am not mistaken in following it." Next morning the prioress opened the box (urged by the two sacristans) and found 500 francs. The prioress told it to Bishop Müller and Bishop Giannattasio in the parlour on 7 Feb 1910, and to Mère Agnès by letter on 25 Feb 1910. A document signed by the community, a commission of priests and the bishop went to Rome. Mgr de Teil told the story at Issy in early 1910 (Laveille). Céline (M-EW-109) mentions the "my way is sure" words at second hand as the link to Thérèse's promise to come back if her way were false.
- The person helped: [OM-007] the Carmel of Gallipoli; Mother Maria Carmela of the Heart of Jesus, prioress [EW-142] the Carmel of Gallipoli (prioress and community; the debt was 300 livres) [EW-109] Carmel of Gallipoli [LF-026] the Carmel of Gallipoli
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-007] the prioress's letter to Mère Agnès, 25 Feb 1910; a document signed by the whole community, a commission of priests and the bishop, sent to Rome; a canonical inquiry by Bishop Müller of Gallipoli, whose Jesuit president (superior of the diocesan seminary) at first called the affair diabolical and was won over. [EW-142] The prioress alone saw it. Giannattasio heard it from Bishop Müller (a few days after) and from the prioress herself on 7 Feb 1910, in Müller's presence. He testified 21 Aug 1911. [EW-109] Céline, second-hand [LF-026] Mgr de Teil; Laveille, who heard him
- Status in the sources: [OM-007] **investigated by the Church** (diocesan canonical inquiry; reported to Rome; not an approved miracle) [EW-142] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process (second-hand); canonical inquiry by Bishop Müller; not an approved miracle [EW-109] mentioned in sworn testimony; not examined here [LF-026] recorded by the shrine (*Pluie de roses*, extract vols. i–ii p.110)
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-142] The prioress had pleurisy and was sweating; the sacristans saw her pale that morning. Giannattasio denied any nervous illness before or after. He does not say she had a doctor's check. A Marcelline sister had mentioned Thérèse shortly before, and the nuns were praying a triduum or novena (F-EW-2456).
- Where the sources differ: Currency: "500 francs" (Taylor 1924, Laveille), "500 lire" (de Teil via Taylor 1926/30, about £20), "500 livres" (Giannattasio); the lira equalled the franc then. The prioress "dreamed" (M-EW-080) or was awake and felt the touch (M-EW-142, Giannattasio, second-hand). The prioress's name is "of the Heart of Jesus" in one source and "of the Sacred Heart of Jesus" in another. The Jesuit president of the tribunal first called the affair diabolical and was won over (Taylor 1924, D6 p. 333). Giannattasio was not present on 16 Jan 1910; he says she gave the "bilocation" detail only under obedience, and he argued against sleepwalking, hidden money and fraud. De Teil says the apparitions were reported to Rome and "to a certain extent influenced" the cause. Bishop Müller tested the prioress with "trials, examinations and vexations".
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [OM-007] currency: "500 francs" (Taylor 1924, Laveille) vs "500 lire" (de Teil via Taylor 1926/30); the lira equalled the franc then (Taylor 1924 note). De Teil says the apparitions were reported to Rome and "to a certain extent influenced" the cause, and that the Bishop of Nardò testified at Lisieux. [EW-142] See M-EW-080 and M-EW-109. Giannattasio was not present on 16 Jan 1910. He reports she was reluctant and gave the "bilocation" detail only under obedience. He argued against sleepwalking, hidden money and fraud (F-EW-2459). [EW-109] Céline says "one has related" it; no detail. [LF-026] The way the notes were placed is not explained.
- Note: Series witnesses (M-EW-080, Articles art. 143, 143a-d): Mother Carmela (letters 25 Feb and Sept 1910); the whole community; de Teil visited 22 Oct 1910 with Canon Cavallera; Bishop Gaetano Müller's canonical inquiry; Bishop Giannattasio testified at the Ordinary Process (21 Aug 1911, PO 33 [1313v]–[1317v]). Status in M-EW-080: canonical inquiry by the Bishop of Gallipoli; testimony at the Ordinary Process; not an approved miracle. Source D3 art. 143, 143a–d, pp. 185–200; D6 pp. 333–340. Medical facts (M-EW-142): the prioress had pleurisy and was sweating; the sacristans saw her pale that morning; Giannattasio denied any nervous illness before or after and does not say a doctor checked her. A Marcelline sister had mentioned Thérèse shortly before and the nuns were praying a triduum or novena. The community's hunger: 1 kg of bread per person per week. Later entries of this series: M-007, M-008, M-009, M-204, M-168.
- Source entries: M-OM-007, M-EW-142, M-EW-109, M-LF-026, M-EW-080 (partial use: M-EW-080)
- Sources cited: [OM-007] Taylor 1924 PDF 375–377 (letters; account "drawn from the Articles for the Cause of Beatification, 2nd edition, July, 1911", PDF 381); Taylor 1926/30 PDF 437–438 (de Teil's version: 500 lire, "about £20"); Laveille PDF 413 (cites *Shower of Roses*, extract from vols. I–II p. 110); Carmel "Les miracles de Thérèse" (« ma voie est sûre, et je ne me suis pas trompée en la suivant »). [EW-142] PO 33, [1313v]–[1317v], W1 (session 86); rights: facts-only [EW-109] PA 8, [779], Session 37 [LF-026] LAV p.413

### M-007 — Gallipoli: further sums of money, January to August 1910
- Date: May 1910
- Status label: investigated by canonical inquiry (same inquiry as M-006); not an approved miracle
- What was reported: Each month-end the Carmel's accounts showed unexplained surpluses (25 lire in January, varying sums February to April). In May Thérèse appeared and said a 50-franc note was in the box ("With God, to say is to do"); two sisters found three notes where there had been two. In June there were 50 lire. On the night of 15–16 Jul (eve of Our Lady of Mount Carmel) she promised 100 lire and gave a 5-lire note, placed at the foot of the Sacred Heart statue. The bishop said he had lost a 100-lire note in his accounts. On 6 Aug (eve of St Cajetan, his name-day) she appeared holding 100 lire, saying "The power of God takes away or gives with the same ease in matters temporal as in matters spiritual". The note was found and sent to the bishop, who returned it. After that alms came from outside and she brought no more. Bishop Müller checked the accounts often and carefully and told Giannattasio the origin of the sums was inexplicable to him.
- The person helped: [EW-143] the Carmel of Gallipoli
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-008] Mother Carmela's letter, September 1910 [EW-143] Reported by Giannattasio from the prioress and Müller; he did not check them himself.
- Status in the sources: [OM-008] **investigated** (same inquiry) [EW-143] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process (second-hand; the witness gives it low weight); not an approved miracle
- Where the sources differ: Giannattasio (M-EW-143) reports the surpluses second-hand from the prioress and Müller and did not check them himself; the process gives his statement low weight.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-143] See M-EW-080 for the monthly sums. The witness says Müller tested the prioress with "trials, examinations and vexations". He also said the community's spiritual growth was extraordinary.
- Source entries: M-OM-008, M-EW-143, M-EW-080 (partial use: M-EW-080)
- Sources cited: [OM-008] Taylor 1924 PDF 378–379; Taylor 1926/30 PDF 437 ("From January till August … mysteriously provided with money") [EW-143] PO 33, [1318r]–[1318v], W1

### M-008 — Gallipoli: prediction on the eve of the exhumation, 5 September 1910
- Date: 5 Sep 1910
- Status label: investigated by canonical inquiry (same inquiry as M-006); not an approved miracle
- What was reported: On 5 Sep 1910, the eve of the first exhumation, of which the prioress knew nothing, Thérèse appeared and said only her bones would be found. She said her relics would work great wonders "as mighty weapons against the devil", and she revealed "great, though hidden, crosses" she bore on earth. Taylor: "They will find only my bones"; it was her wish that her dust be scattered to win love.
- Status in the sources: [OM-009] **investigated**
- Where the sources differ: Taylor 1924 PDF 379 and Taylor 1926/30 PDF 271, 437 give the prediction. M-EW-080 says the prediction was that only her bones would be found.
- Source entries: M-OM-009, M-EW-080 (partial use: M-EW-080)
- Sources cited: [OM-009] Taylor 1924 PDF 379; Taylor 1926/30 PDF 271, 437

### M-009 — Gallipoli: the bishop's sealed envelope, 16 January 1911
- Date: 16 Jan 1911
- Status label: investigated by canonical inquiry (Bishop Müller); sworn by the sender, Bishop Giannattasio, at the Ordinary Process; not an approved miracle
- What was reported: Mgr Nicola Giannattasio, Bishop of Nardò, put a 500-lire note and his card ("My Way is a Sure one: I am not mistaken ... Gallipoli, January 16, 1910") in an envelope, sealed it inside a larger one, to be opened at Gallipoli on 16 Jan 1911. The nuns had been making a novena for 300 lire to decorate the chapel (altar flowers). At the parlour grille he saw the prioress open a corner. The seal was intact and the gum folds were as he had made them. Inside the thin inner envelope were two 100-lire and two 50-lire notes (300 lire), with his own small envelope and 500 note untouched in the middle. One 50-lire note smelled lightly of roses; the bishop exchanged it for another note. He was convinced it was supernatural and "moved to tears" that evening. He saw in it a confirmation of "My way is a sure one" as her doctrine.
- The person helped: [EW-144] the Carmel of Gallipoli (the nuns had asked for 300 livres for altar flowers)
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-010] account in the Articles for the Cause (1911) [EW-144] Giannattasio (only direct witness of the opening, with the prioress); he swore a statement to Müller at the end of the retreat and again at the process on 22 Aug 1911.
- Status in the sources: [OM-010] **investigated** (Bishop Müller's inquiry) [EW-144] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process (first-hand, by the sender); not an approved miracle
- Where the sources differ: Giannattasio corrected an ambiguity in Article 145b: he did know the "My way is sure" words when he sent the card (F-EW-2471). He swore a statement to Müller at the end of the retreat and again at the process on 22 Aug 1911. The folios [1320]–[1323r] fall between the two parts of the text on the site and are not in the extract.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-144] See M-EW-080 (total of 300 lire, one note smelling of roses). Giannattasio corrected an ambiguity in Article 145b: he did know the "My way is sure" words when he sent the card (F-EW-2471). The folios [1320]–[1323r] fall between the two parts of the text on the site and do not appear in this extract.
- Source entries: M-OM-010, M-EW-144, M-EW-080 (partial use: M-EW-080)
- Sources cited: [OM-010] Taylor 1924 PDF 380–381; Taylor 1926/30 PDF 437–438 [EW-144] PO 33, [1318v]–[1319v] and [1323v]–[1326v], W1 (sessions 86–87)

## B2. Investigated by the Church: presented to the process as a case (vice-postulator's Articles 1910 or Mère Agnès's 1915 summary), not approved

### M-010 — Mme Héloïse Debossu, Marnes-la-Coquette (Reims): fibrous tumour and peritonitis, September 1901
- Date: Sep 1901
- Status label: listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910); recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: Mme Héloïse Debossu had a fibrous tumour on the left side for about ten years and refused an operation. Massage and electricity gave passing relief. She was bedridden from January 1901, and had peritonitis in September; the doctors gave her up. Abbé D. Petit, curé of Marnes-la-Coquette (former director of the seminary of Versailles), sent a lock of Thérèse's hair and asked the Carmel for a novena. On the last day of the novena she walked to church for Communion, and her health and weight returned. Taylor counts it among "the first hundreds of cures reported by lay persons".
- The person helped: [EW-037] Mme Héloïse Debossu, 9 rue Luiquet (formerly 5 avenue de Laon), Reims [LF-023] Mme Debossu [OM-013] Mme Debossu, Marnes-la-Coquette (Seine-et-Oise)
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-037] Abbé D. Petit [LF-023] Laveille (footnote citing the *Pluie de roses*)
- Status in the sources: [EW-037] listed in the vice-postulator's Articles (1910) as a miracle to be proved; not approved [LF-023] recorded by the shrine [OM-013] recorded by the Carmel ("amongst the first hundreds of cures reported by lay persons")
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-037] fibroid tumour; peritonitis; operation advised, refused [LF-023] fibrous tumour; peritonitis [OM-013] fibrous tumour; peritonitis.
- Where the sources differ: Year of the peritonitis: September 1901 (Laveille, Taylor) or September 1902 (M-EW-037, D3 art. 121). Place: Marnes-la-Coquette (Laveille, Taylor); M-EW-037 gives her address as 9 rue Luiquet (formerly 5 avenue de Laon), Reims. No doctor's certificate is quoted in D3.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-037] no doctor's certificate quoted [LF-023] Details in the *Pluie de roses* volumes (not seen).
- Source entries: M-EW-037, M-LF-023, M-OM-013
- Sources cited: [EW-037] D3 art. 121 pp. 134–135 (PDF 148–149) [LF-023] LAV p.412 fn. [OM-013] Laveille PDF 412 note 2

### M-011 — Mme Jouanne, Marnes-la-Coquette, Jan 1903
- Date: Jan 1903
- Status label: listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910)
- What was reported: A gardener's wife, mother of two, previously operated for double strangulated hernia; then appendicitis with general peritonitis; a Paris surgeon opened the abdomen, found abscess and diffuse pus, closed it, gave hours to live. The curé slipped under her bolster a sachet of rose petals with which Thérèse had caressed her crucifix; the same day the illness stopped; out of danger on the third day; seen in good health in 1907 at Versailles.
- The person helped: Mme Jouanne
- Witnesses and reporter: Abbé D. Petit, letters 23 Jan 1903 and 23 Jul 1907
- Status in the sources: in the Articles; not approved
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): failed laparotomy; peritonitis
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): no certificate
- Source entries: M-EW-038
- Sources cited: D3 art. 122 pp. 135–138 (PDF 149–152)

### M-012 — Marthe Bourgon, Nancy, 1905
- Date: May 1905
- Status label: listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910)
- What was reported: Appendicitis diagnosed late; operation curtailed because of gangrene; a sinus to the intestine formed; dying; a lock of Thérèse's hair and a novena; two days later the sinus closed; the chief surgeon: "This cure is a phenomenon."
- The person helped: Mlle Marthe Bourgon, 19
- Witnesses and reporter: M. Robert, letter Nancy 5 May 1905
- Status in the sources: in the Articles
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): surgery with gangrene; fistula
- Source entries: M-EW-042
- Sources cited: D3 art. 123 pp. 138–139 (PDF 152–153)

### M-013 — Brother Ignatius Boron SJ, Cracow: liver stones cured, March 1906
- Date: 20 Mar 1906
- Status label: listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910); recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: Brother Ignatius Boron, a Jesuit lay brother at Cracow, had liver stones from Christmas 1905. Professors Parenski and Domanski called the illness incurable and the surgeon Kader said an operation was indispensable. A novena was made through Thérèse. There was a crisis on day 2 and he was cured on day 3 (20 Mar 1906). Fr Casimir Konopka SJ reported it on 19 May 1906.
- The person helped: [LF-024] unnamed Jesuit lay brother
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-043] Fr Casimir Konopka SJ, 19 May 1906 [LF-024] Laveille (footnote)
- Status in the sources: [EW-043] in the Articles [LF-024] recorded by the shrine [OM-015] recorded by the Carmel
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [LF-024] "serious liver disease"
- Where the sources differ: Laveille and Taylor (M-LF-024, M-OM-015) give only "a serious liver disease" and "March 1906" without the name; M-EW-043 (Articles art. 124) gives the name, the professors and the surgeon.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [LF-024] No further details.
- Source entries: M-EW-043, M-LF-024, M-OM-015
- Sources cited: [EW-043] D3 art. 124 p. 139 (PDF 153) [LF-024] LAV p.412 fn. [OM-015] Laveille PDF 412 note 2

### M-014 — Sister Joséphine, Carmel of Nîmes in exile, Florence, Jan 1907
- Date: Jan 1907
- Status label: listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910)
- What was reported: Lay sister, 41; infectious double lobar pneumonia after influenza from 18 Jan; 43 °C; Cheyne-Stokes breathing; pulse >150; near anuria; the prioress, woken at 2 a.m., found her black and glazed; refused the prayers for the dying, said the Creed, felt "as if touched"; crisis passed; the sister said she had not prayed for her cure and had not wanted it.
- Witnesses and reporter: the prioress; Prof. Maestro (two declarations)
- Status in the sources: in the Articles
- Source entries: M-EW-045
- Sources cited: D3 art. 126 pp. 143–145 (PDF 157–159)

### M-015 — Louis Legot, Lisieux: child of five cured of meningitis, March 1908
- Date: Mar 1908
- Status label: sworn at the Church process; listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910)
- What was reported: Louis Legot, five, of Lisieux, had meningitis in March 1908 and was delirious and speechless for hours; the doctor declared it incurable. His mother asked as a sign that he would ask for a drink when she came home from Mass. He did, and he recovered. Abbé J. Lamy of Saint-Jacques reported it on 16 Apr 1909, and Maupas repeated Lamy's account at the Ordinary Process (Articles art. 131 and 132).
- The person helped: [EW-136] "le jeune Legot", Lisieux
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-136] Abbé Lamy, curate of Saint-Jacques, told Maupas his own observations
- Status in the sources: [EW-049] in the Articles [EW-136] listed in the vice-postulator's Articles, no. 132; sworn testimony (hearsay)
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-136] meningitis; declared incurable by the doctor
- Where the sources differ: M-EW-136 (Maupas, hearsay from Lamy) gives only "meningitis declared incurable by the doctor, and was cured"; M-EW-049 gives the drink sign. The two entries use different article numbers (131 and 132) for what is the same case.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-136] Details are in the Articles, not in this deposition.
- Source entries: M-EW-049, M-EW-136
- Sources cited: [EW-049] D3 art. 131 pp. 155–156 (PDF 169–170) [EW-136] PO-25, f. 1231r–1231v

### M-016 — Sister Sainte-Foy (Louise Chincholle), Carmel of Rodez, 1908
- Date: Apr 1908
- Status label: listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910)
- What was reported: Six years of weakness, 16 months total loss of voice, blood-spitting (feared tubercular laryngitis); novena to the Holy Face through Thérèse from 26 Apr 1908; day 2 hummed two lines of "Vivre d'amour"; cured day 4.
- Status in the sources: in the Articles
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): doctor (27 Jul 1908) notes signs of "neuropathy" (loss of corneal and pharyngeal reflex) — a natural explanation the source itself records
- Source entries: M-EW-046
- Sources cited: D3 art. 127 pp. 145–147 (PDF 159–161)

### M-017 — Reine Fauquet, Lisieux: eye disease cured and a vision of Thérèse, 26 May 1908 ("blind child of four")
- Date: 26 May 1908
- Status label: listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910); recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: Reine Fauquet, four and a half, of Lisieux, had phlyctenular keratitis since 11 Jan 1906. Dr D. (Lisieux) and Drs M. and L. (Caen) said her eyes were hopeless. Mme Saint-Edmond of the Providence Sisters urged the mother to carry her to the grave, because "Reine" was Louis Martin's name for Thérèse. On 26 May 1908, after a crisis, the child said she no longer needed her glasses. Her sister Marie (9½) saw her smile and wave at something. Reine said: "I saw my little Teresa ... She took my hand ... she had a veil ... bright round her head", "dressed like you" (like the nuns). Laveille and Taylor summarize it as the instantaneous cure of a "little blind child of four to whom the saint appeared". Certificates: Dr D. 6 Jul 1908; Dr La Néele 7 Dec 1908 (cured completely); parents' signed account 5 Feb 1909.
- The person helped: [LF-025] a child, unnamed
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-050] Dr La Néele (certificate 7 Dec 1908); parents' signed account 5 Feb 1909; Dr D. certificate 6 Jul 1908 [LF-025] Laveille (footnote)
- Status in the sources: [EW-050] in the Articles [OM-017] recorded by the Carmel [LF-025] recorded by the shrine
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-050] keratitis tends to relapse and leave scars; the certificate says she was cured completely [LF-025] blindness (cause not stated)
- Where the sources differ: Laveille calls her "blind"; D3 gives phlyctenular keratitis with glasses. Age: four (Laveille) or four and a half (D3). D3 notes that keratitis tends to relapse and leave scars. Isabelle (M-EW-201) mentions a visit by Reine Fauquet to the Carmel parlour later.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [LF-025] No further details.
- Source entries: M-EW-050, M-OM-017, M-LF-025
- Sources cited: [EW-050] D3 art. 128 pp. 147–150 (PDF 161–164) [OM-017] Laveille PDF 412 note 2 [LF-025] LAV p.412 fn.

- What was reported: TB diagnosed 19 Apr 1908 by Dr Joseph Marquis, "two years at most"; novenas ending 30 Sept; certified well 15 Nov 1908 though she had had the usual treatment ("strange and marvellous"); re-attested March 1910.

- Date: 21 Feb 1909

- What was reported: Nurse, 24; stomach ulcer, haematemesis; relapse 8 Feb 1909; artificial feeding; wool from Thérèse's pillow in a sachet; at the third Ave after Mass felt a hand placed on the painful part; drank milk, ate chocolate and bread; Dr Tison (18 May 1909) confirmed; he noted no nervous history.

### M-020 — Sr Mary of Calvary (Marie du Calvaire), Carmel of Mangalore, India: pneumonia cured, March 1909
- Date: 24 Mar 1909
- Status label: sworn at the Church process; listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910); recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: Sr Mary of Calvary, aged 66, of the Carmel of Mangalore, had pneumonia with liver and kidney disease. She received Viaticum on 16 Mar 1909, wanted to die and reproached the doctor. She heard a voice telling her to abandon herself. A novena followed, with a suffocation crisis on 23 Mar. At 3.30 a.m. on 24 Mar she felt comfort, got up and knelt half an hour at Communion. Dr L. P. Fernandez certified on 31 Jul 1909. Fr Élie cited the printed report (Pluie de roses no. LXXXIX, letter of the Mangalore prioress, 7 Jun 1909) at the Ordinary Process.
- The person helped: [EW-057] Sr Marie du Calvaire, Carmel of Mangalore, India
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-057] prioress of Mangalore, by letter; Élie only read it
- Status in the sources: [EW-063] in the Articles [EW-057] printed account cited in sworn testimony
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-057] pneumonia complicated by liver disease and a kidney disorder
- Where the sources differ: M-EW-057 is Élie's citation of the printed account; Élie vouches only for the fervour of that Carmel. M-EW-063 is the Articles account with the certificate.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-057] Élie vouches only for the fervour of that Carmel (he met its founder, Mère Élie from Pau, at Beypore station in 1870).
- Source entries: M-EW-063, M-EW-057
- Sources cited: [EW-063] D3 art. 132 pp. 156–162 (PDF 170–176) [EW-057] PO-05, f. 449r

### M-024 — Sister Marie-Bénigne Martin, Visitation of Caen, 2 July 1909 (Léonie's intervention)
- Date: 2 Jul 1909
- Status label: listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910)
- What was reported: Lay sister; stomach ulcer from Dec 1908, vomiting blood; novena from 24 Jun; on the last day at 1 p.m. Léonie (Sr Françoise-Thérèse) gave her water with a rose petal Thérèse had used on her crucifix; pain ended; drank a litre of milk; next day omelette, peas, salad. Doctor's certificate 29 Jul 1909.
- The person helped: Sr Marie-Bénigne Martin (surname as printed; relation to the family not stated)
- Status in the sources: in the Articles
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): Léonie as the agent — valuable family link.
- Source entries: M-EW-067
- Sources cited: D3 art. 135 pp. 171–173 (PDF 185–187)

### M-025 — Mary Antes, New York: trampled by a horse, 30 July 1909
- Date: 30 Jul 1909
- Status label: listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910); recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: On 30 Jul 1909 a woman (Mary Antes) was knocked down and trampled by a frightened horse in a New York street. Her face was bruised, her head bled, ribs were broken and driven into the lung, and the heart was injured. She confessed in the street. The ambulance doctor gave "one in a thousand". After a nun's advice a novena began on 3 Aug and a relic (picture-relic) was applied. She mended and on the last day was out of danger. Her sister, Sr Antonia OP of the Holy Cross convent, Brooklyn ("Sister M. A." in Taylor), reported it on 12 Aug 1909.
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-021] "Sister M. A.", her sister, New York, 12 Aug 1909
- Status in the sources: [EW-068] in the Articles [OM-021] recorded by the Carmel
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [OM-021] hospital; ambulance doctor.
- Where the sources differ: M-EW-068 records that there is no medical certificate.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-068] no medical certificate
- Source entries: M-EW-068, M-OM-021
- Sources cited: [EW-068] D3 art. 136 pp. 173–174 (PDF 187–188) [OM-021] Taylor 1924 PDF 402–403

### M-028 — Henriette Luchini, Piacenza: enteritis cured, and the lost parcel of relics, 1909

CXX.

### M-031 — Abbé Claude-Marcel Weber, Saint-Jean-de-Luz: cataract, sight restored after three novenas
- Date: May 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process; listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910); recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: Abbé Weber, a priest of Saint-Jean-de-Luz aged 75 or 76, had a beginning cataract seen by Dr Baradat of Cannes in spring 1900 and confirmed in September 1900 by an assistant of the oculist Abadie in Paris. He took no treatment. His sight failed until he could not recognize friends at 12 paces. In May 1909 a travelling optician called the right eye "completely extinguished". On Palm Sunday 1909 he fell down the altar steps. The exiled Carmel of Bordeaux at Zarautz (he advised the nuns on bees) made novenas to Thérèse without telling him. A few days into the first, he read easily and saw the altar steps. After the second (begun 19 May, with a picture-relic laid on his eyes each evening) he read the tiny print of the Decrees at the front of his breviary and recognized people at more than a hundred paces. During the third he could again see bee eggs in a queen cell with the naked eye. He said his sight was back to its state before 1899, better than normal for his age. Letter of 23 Jul 1910; attestation of the Zarautz prioress 10 Aug 1910; Dr Baradat's letter of 7 Sept 1910; he testified at the Ordinary Process on 22–23 Mar 1911.
- The person helped: [EW-079] Abbé Claude-Marcel Weber, 75–76
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-079] Weber; the Zarauz prioress Sr Thérèse-Aimée du Cœur de Jésus and her council (signed attestation, 10 August 1910); letter of Dr Baradat, 7 September 1910 [OM-022] the priest himself ("Rev. Chas. W…", name garbled in the scan), 23 Jul 1910
- Status in the sources: [EW-055] in the Articles [EW-079] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process; not an approved miracle [OM-022] recorded by the Carmel
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-079] Spring 1900: Dr Baradat of Cannes, consulted for anaemia, saw a beginning cataract. September 1900: an assistant of the oculist Abbadie (boulevard Saint-Germain, Paris) confirmed it in a dark room with electric light and said to come back in ten years for a free operation when it was "ripe". No treatment was taken after that. From 1906 he read and wrote only with difficulty, even with strong glasses; a "veil" thickened. From early 1908, in daylight, he could not recognize close friends at 12 paces; he dared not go out at dusk. May 1909: a travelling optician declared the right eye "completely extinguished" and the other very sick; Weber says he still saw a vague shadow with the right eye at two paces. Palm Sunday 1909 he fell down the choir steps in front of the parish. He feared he could no longer say Mass or his breviary. Dr Baradat's 1910 letter recalls "an opacity, a cloud" in one lens (left or right, he forgot) seen ten years before, too vague for a certificate. [OM-022] cataract diagnosis; no operation; no remedy used.
- Where the sources differ: Year of the novenas: Taylor (M-OM-022) puts the second novena "from 19 May 1909"; the deposition (M-EW-079) puts all three novenas in March, May and June 1910 and the fall on Palm Sunday 1909. Name: Taylor prints "Rev. Chas. W…" (garbled); the deposition gives Claude-Marcel Weber. Age: 67 in 1900 (Taylor) means about 76; deposition says 75–76. First symptoms: 1899 (Weber) but first diagnosis spring 1900. Weber dates the first improvement to March 1910. No medical certificate before or after the cure; the promoter asked for a certificate of public knowledge. The cure was gradual over three novenas.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-079] No medical certificate before or after the cure; the promoter asked for a certificate of public knowledge instead. Cure was gradual over three novenas, not instant. Weber says symptoms began in 1899, but his first diagnosis was spring 1900. Weber dates the first improvement to March 1910 and the first novena is dated March by the prioress, though he calls it "spring". A human detail: Weber was a passionate beekeeper; he had to give up his bees; the Zarauz prioress, who had bought some colonies, wanted him to come and place an Italian queen, and said: "We are going to do violence to the good God, and he will be obliged to give you back your sight."
- Source entries: M-EW-055, M-EW-079, M-OM-022
- Sources cited: [EW-055] D3 art. 144 pp. 200–205 (PDF 214–219) [EW-079] PO-19, f. 1130r–1134v and 1138v, sessions 69–70, 22–23 March 1911 [OM-022] Taylor 1924 PDF 405–407

### M-032 — Ferdinand Aubry, Lisieux: ulcer of the tongue (feared cancer) cured with a rose petal, September–October 1910
- Date: 2 Oct 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process; listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910); recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- Flag: M-EW-111 does not name the man; the link to Aubry is inferred from the place and the illness
- What was reported: Ferdinand Aubry, 60, lived in the home of the Little Sisters of the Poor at Lisieux. He had a gangrenous ulcer of the tongue (feared cancer). He swallowed a rose petal from Thérèse's deathbed on 28 Sept 1910 (Laveille: one of the petals she had touched to the crucifix on 14 Sep 1897). On 2 Oct he said "I am cured". The missing part of the tongue grew back in three weeks (a photograph is attached to the doctor's certificate). He died peacefully on 18 Dec 1910. Dr La Néele also told Jeanne (M-EW-111) of a cure of "cancer of the tongue" in an old man of a house of the Little Sisters of the Poor (unnamed).
- The person helped: [LF-014] Ferdinand Aubry, old man [EW-111] an elderly man in a house of the Little Sisters of the Poor (unnamed)
- Witnesses and reporter: [LF-014] Laveille (footnote) [EW-111] Dr La Néele via Jeanne
- Status in the sources: [EW-082] in the Articles [LF-014] recorded by the monastery [EW-111] hearsay in sworn testimony at the Apostolic Process; not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [LF-014] "cancer on the tongue" (Laveille's words) [EW-111] cancer of the tongue
- Where the sources differ: Laveille calls it "cancer on the tongue"; the Articles call it a gangrenous ulcer (feared cancer). Laveille puts the cure in September 1910; D3 puts the petal on 28 Sept and the cure on 2 Oct.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [LF-014] No doctor named. [EW-111] no names, dates or place
- Source entries: M-EW-082, M-LF-014, M-EW-111
- Sources cited: [EW-082] D3 art. 147 pp. 213–218 (PDF 227–232); LAV p. 372 n. (PDF 392) [LF-014] LAV p.392 fn. [EW-111] PA-23 [1360]

### M-034 — Abbé (Canon) Jules Rossignol, Beaupréau: apparition and peace before death, 16–18 January 1911
- Date: 16 Jan 1911
- Status label: sworn at the Church process; listed in the Articles of the Cause (1910); recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: Abbé Jules Rossignol, about 76, was for about 26 years professor of dogmatic and moral theology at the Luçon major seminary and for about four years lived in the retired priests' house of Saint-Martin, Beaupréau (near Angers). For over forty years he had been secretly terrified of God's judgment (Taylor: he scourged himself until the walls were blood-spattered). On waking on the morning of 16 Jan 1911 he saw "little Sister Thérèse" at his bedside, as in her photograph, smiling. She made him understand by signs and her whole face that she was taking care of "his affair" and that it would come soon ("I am seeing to it ... all will come right"; "count on me"). He said: "It is not a dream, I was fully awake." He told Abbé Frapereau (asking him to tell no one) and his confessor Fr Marie-Arsène, Trappist of Bellefontaine. His fear gave way to joy. He died almost suddenly two days later, on 18 Jan 1911, offering his life with extraordinary joy and no sign of fear.
- The person helped: [EW-089] Abbé Jules Rossignol, about 76, for about 26 years professor of dogmatic and moral theology at the Luçon major seminary; for about four years in the retired priests' house of Saint-Martin, Beaupréau
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-089] Frapereau (heard it from Rossignol; present at his death); Fr Marie-Arsène, Trappist of Bellefontaine (written attestation inserted in the acts); Gaignet (heard from Frapereau and knew Rossignol's fears) [OM-029] Fr Frappereau; Fr Arsenius, Trappist of Bellefontaine (his confessor); account in the Articles for the Cause; notes by de Teil
- Status in the sources: [EW-088] in the Articles [EW-089] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process; not an approved miracle [OM-029] recorded by the Carmel (printed in the Articles for the Cause)
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-089] Rossignol had long had a chronic intestinal disorder, but took full part in community life; nothing foretold his almost sudden death two days after the apparition (i.e. 18 January 1911).
- Where the sources differ: Nobody asked him what the "affair" was. Two readings: (1) reassurance against his exaggerated terror of judgment (known to Arsène and Gaignet, not to Frapereau); (2) his nephew Abbé Camille Rossignol wrote that he had founded a Christian school paid from his own money and "he had to die so that his school might live". Gaignet stressed his humility, sober mind and no taste for exaggerated mysticism. Rossignol knelt two hours daily before the Blessed Sacrament; instruments of penance were found after his death.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-089] Nobody asked him what the "affair" was; "everyone regretted" it (Gaignet). Two readings: (1) reassurance against his exaggerated terror of God's judgment (known to Arsène and Gaignet, not to Frapereau); (2) his nephew Abbé Camille Rossignol wrote that he had founded a Christian school paid from his own money, and living longer drained it: "he had to die so that his school might live." Gaignet stressed Rossignol's humility, sober mind, and no taste for exaggerated mysticism; the judge asked if he was still lucid at the end; Gaignet said yes, citing Frapereau and the nephew. Rossignol knelt two full hours daily before the Blessed Sacrament; instruments of penance were found after his death.
- Source entries: M-EW-088, M-EW-089, M-OM-029
- Sources cited: [EW-088] D3 art. 145 pp. 205–208 (PDF 219–222) [EW-089] PO-21, f. 1162r–1164v, session 72, 30 March 1911; PO-22, f. 1166r–1167v, sessions 72–73 [OM-029] Taylor 1924 PDF 434–436

### M-035 — Clémentine Derenne ("C. D."), 17, Laval: albuminuria and meningitis, three apparitions, 2–4 February 1911
- Date: 2 Feb 1911
- Status label: listed in Mère Agnès's 1915 summary of 54 dossiers; recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: Clémentine Derenne, 17, of Laval, had albuminuria threatening phthisis. She took cerebral uraemia on 24 Jan 1911, and on 1 Feb the doctor said meningitis had set in. On 2 Feb, given a relic (wool from Thérèse's pillow), she could read its tiny print. At midday she saw a young nun who said "You will be cured" and she smelled a perfume; she recognized people again. At 3 p.m. a second vision asked her to say the Rosary daily. At 6 p.m. the doctor (Dr Pivert of Laval) found no trace of lung trouble or meningitis. A third vision on 4 Feb at 8 p.m. spoke of personal matters. She made a pilgrimage to the tomb on 14 Aug 1911. Mgr de Teil made an inquiry and confirmed the facts.
- The person helped: [EW-148] Clémentine Derenne, 17, Laval, Mayenne
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-030] C. D., 5 Nov 1911, based on accounts of her confessor Fr G., her mother, aunt (Mlle D.) and Mme B. [EW-148] The doctor(s) below wrote certificates; Mère Agnès read the summary aloud at the Apostolic Process. She was not a direct witness.
- Status in the sources: [OM-030] recorded by the Carmel [EW-148] listed by Mère Agnès in her summary of 54 dossiers, handed with the originals to the tribunal (19 Jul 1915); not selected as an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [OM-030] morphia injections planned; consultation cancelled. [EW-148] albuminuria, meningitis and pulmonary phthisis. Dr Pivert of Laval saw the cure the same day.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-148] Summary by a partisan witness; the dossiers themselves are not in our sources. Date of the event: 2 Feb 1911.
- Source entries: M-OM-030, M-EW-148
- Sources cited: [OM-030] Taylor 1924 PDF 409–411 [EW-148] PA 6 (Mère Agnès), [536], S21 19 Jul 1915, item 12 of her 54-case summary, W2; rights: facts-only; she cites: Pluie de roses I–II p. 69

### M-036 — The Rev. Alexander James Grant, Edinburgh: sense of Thérèse's presence and conversion, 1909 to 20 April 1911
- Date: 20 Apr 1911
- Status label: listed in the Articles of the Cause (art. 148; a conversion, not a healing); sworn at both processes (witness 27); recorded by the Carmel
- What was reported: Alexander James Grant, a United Free Church minister (Loch Ranza, Arran, later Edinburgh), read the English Story of a Soul. One evening late in 1909, fully awake and ill with influenza, he felt an extraordinary sensation and told his wife: "That little girl is here... the Little Flower... I am sure she is in this room." The outer world seemed to vanish and he "saw" her inwardly. He seemed to hear "This is how Catholic saints love Jesus; choose my little way." In August 1910, amid rationalist doubts, he felt her near again. He bought her Life in French in September 1910, on the very day a novena for him (unknown to him) ended. He heard "Why do you ask me to pray for you and ignore the Blessed Virgin?" and began to pray to Mary. At the end of March 1911, in a convent chapel during a novena to Thérèse, he felt Christ's presence in the tabernacle: "Now I believe he is there". He was received into the Church on 20 Apr 1911 by Fr Widdowson SJ (names Francis Mary Teresa); letter of 23 Apr 1911. His conversion is shown in a window of the Carmel chapel. The Grants later became guardians of the Alençon birthplace; he died there in July 1917. Money from Glasgow after the conversion: M-199.
- The person helped: [EW-060] Alexander James Grant [LF-027] Rev. Alexander Grant
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-060] Grant; his wife (present at the first); the nun who taught him French [LF-027] Grant (letter of 23 Apr 1911, *Pluie de roses* extract i–ii p.118)
- Status in the sources: [EW-090] conversion; testified at the Ordinary Process (witness 27) [EW-060] sworn testimony at both processes; Langlois says it weighed heavily in the cause [LF-027] recorded by the shrine
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-060] first experience while ill with influenza
- Where the sources differ: The 1915 account (PA-05) is shorter and more gradual; the 1911 account (PO-27) more visionary. Wording of the message: "choose my little way" (M-EW-060, M-LF-027) or "Follow me, my way is sure" (M-EW-090). Langlois says the testimony weighed heavily in the cause. Grant says nothing in his upbringing prepared him.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-060] Grant says nothing in his upbringing or reading prepared him (he had read only St Teresa of Avila's Life and one 16th-century author, 30 years before). The 1915 account is shorter and more gradual; the 1911 account more visionary. [LF-027] Dates as printed (baptism 20 Apr, letter 23 Apr).
- Source entries: M-EW-090, M-EW-060, M-LF-027, M-OM-059
- Sources cited: [EW-090] D3 art. 148 pp. 218–222 (PDF 232–236); D4 ch. XII (PDF 186–189) [EW-060] PO-27, f. 1251r–1255v, session 80, 8 August 1911; PA-05, p. 312–314, 1915 [LF-027] LAV p.416–417; F-LF-253

### M-047 — Aline Poirson, Anrosey (Aurosay), Haute-Marne: liver and spleen disease cured, 1 July 1912
- Date: 1 Jul 1912
- Status label: listed in Mère Agnès's 1915 summary of 54 dossiers; recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: Aline Poirson (55 in the Articles account, 57 in 1915), of Anrosey near Chaumont, had been ill since 20 and unable to eat meat or bread for 19 years. She was bedridden five and a half years, with liver and spleen swollen to four or five times normal size, kept alive by injections (a litre of ether a week), and her right hand was useless for nine months. A relic arrived on 15 Jun 1912 and a novena began on 19 Jun. The vomiting stopped that night. At 2 a.m. on 1 Jul 1912 she felt well and heard: "Get up; you are cured ... go and see your children". She dressed alone at 4 a.m. and walked to Mass at 5.30. She ate meat that day, managed her daughter's hotel, and gained 45 lb by 15 Sep. Her priest said "You are mad!" and she answered "No, Father, I am cured!" Dr Vauthrin met her on the way; Dr Malingre (who had treated her from Nov 1891) found the liver normal on 2 Jul 1912 and called the cure "absolutely miraculous". She made a thanksgiving pilgrimage to Lisieux in July 1915. Taylor calls it "the astounding miracle at Chaumont".
- The person helped: [EW-159] Mme Poirson, 57, Anrosey, Haute-Marne
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-041] Dr Vauthrin (met her on the way); Dr A. Malingre (treated her from Nov 1891; sent her to Vichy 1893 and 1896, where Dr Willemin judged her incurable; last saw her 17 Mar 1907 expecting death within a fortnight; found the liver normal on 2 Jul 1912); her parish priest (October 1912) [EW-159] The doctor(s) below wrote certificates; Mère Agnès read the summary aloud at the Apostolic Process. She was not a direct witness.
- Status in the sources: [OM-041] recorded by the Carmel (Taylor: "the astounding miracle at Chaumont") [EW-159] listed by Mère Agnès in her summary of 54 dossiers, handed with the originals to the tribunal (19 Jul 1915); not selected as an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [OM-041] morphia; strict diet; ether and turpentine; boldo; salicylate of soda; Vichy water and cure; gallstones. [EW-159] incurable liver condition. Dr Vauthrin of Anrosey, 20 Jul 1913: the cure came suddenly, without any action of the medical art. Dr Malingre of Chaumont, 6 Aug 1912: 'absolutely miraculous'.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-159] Summary by a partisan witness; the dossiers themselves are not in our sources. Date of the event: 1 Jul 1912.
- Source entries: M-OM-041, M-EW-159
- Sources cited: [OM-041] Taylor 1924 PDF 416–420 [EW-159] PA 6 (Mère Agnès), [539]–[540], S21 19 Jul 1915, item 23 of her 54-case summary, W2; rights: facts-only; she cites: Pluie de roses III p. 370 no. 443

Dr Hérébel of Lambezellec, 28 May 1914: complete, definitive cure in a month of a serious lesion that is very slow to heal normally. He was surprised and said so to her before he knew of any supernatural claim. He concludes that natural healing was possible, but the speed was extraordinary and far beyond what normal treatment allowed.

### M-073 — Private Julien Viquesnel, Limoges, 3 Nov 1914
- Date: 3 Nov 1914
- Status label: listed in Mère Agnès's 1915 summary of 54 dossiers
- What was reported: Held to be lost, he prayed with faith to Sister Thérèse. On 3 Nov, while his wife made a pilgrimage for him to the tomb, the many clamps holding his torn jaw and ear fell off without being touched. From then he could move and eat, and all danger ended.
- The person helped: Julien Viquesnel, soldier, of Fontaine-la-Louvet (Eure), Hospital of Limoges
- Witnesses and reporter: The doctor(s) below wrote certificates; Mère Agnès read the summary aloud at the Apostolic Process. She was not a direct witness.
- Status in the sources: listed by Mère Agnès in her summary of 54 dossiers, handed with the originals to the tribunal (19 Jul 1915); not selected as an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): a bullet entered behind the head, shattered the jaw, left through the nose and half-cut the carotid artery. Report signed by the nurse, the soldier, two priest-nurses (one called it 'truly extraordinary') and two hospital sisters; sent by the Carmel of Limoges; medical certificate of Dr Rousseau attached.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): Summary by a partisan witness; the dossiers themselves are not in our sources. Date of the event: 3 Nov 1914.
- Source entries: M-EW-189
- Sources cited: PA 6 (Mère Agnès), [550]–[551], S21 19 Jul 1915, item 53 of her 54-case summary, W2; rights: facts-only

- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): leg wound (tibia pierced, fibula broken), general infection and haemorrhages. Report of 15 Dec 1914 signed by his mother, the hospital superior, two male nurses and a nurse. Dr Deneleau, head doctor, added: he was still amazed and could not explain how the man was alive; 'we count for so little in such cases'.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): Summary by a partisan witness; the dossiers themselves are not in our sources. Date of the event: 5 Nov 1914.
