### M-150 — Sr Marie-Madeleine du Saint-Sacrement (Mélanie Lebon), Lisieux Carmel: about 35 boils cured after a second novena, May–June 1908
- Date: Jun 1908
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: From September 1907 a leg illness kept the lay sister Marie-Madeleine (born 1869) in the infirmary for eight months: an unbroken series of abscesses or boils (35 in eight months by her count), with wasting of the limb and general decline. In mid-May 1908 the prioress, Mère Marie-Ange, proposed that she call on Sister Thérèse so as to return to work in the kitchen. During the first novena the illness rather grew worse; a second novena brought complete cure. On the first Sunday of June 1908 she took up her kitchen work and did all the cooking alone from then on, though white-veil sisters normally shared it in a weekly turn. Céline (PO 4) links the cure to a veil believed, but not surely, to have been worn by Thérèse. Marie des Anges (second-hand, 1915) adds that Céline could not tell which was Thérèse's great veil and asked Thérèse for a sign: the veil laid on the leg of a white-veil sister covered with about thirty boils would cure her.
- The person helped: [EW-193] Sr Marie-Madeleine du Saint-Sacrement [EW-194] Sr Marie-Madeleine du Saint-Sacrement (Mélanie Lebon), lay sister, born 1869 [EW-203] a white-veil (lay) sister, unnamed in this text (Sr Marie-Madeleine per M-EW-194)
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-193] Céline (reporter) [EW-194] herself, sworn 16 Mar 1911 (session 67); the prioress Marie-Ange proposed the novena [EW-203] Marie des Anges, second-hand, 9–10 Sep 1915
- Status in the sources: [EW-193] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process (second-hand); not an approved miracle [EW-194] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process; not an approved miracle [EW-203] sworn testimony at the Apostolic Process (second-hand); not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-193] furunculosis; no doctor named. [EW-194] 35 abscesses/boils over eight months; wasting of the leg; general decline. No doctor named in the text. [EW-203] about thirty boils on the leg; no doctor named.
- Where the sources differ: Number of boils: about 30 (Céline, Marie des Anges) or 35 (the patient). The veil: Céline and Marie des Anges mention it; the patient does not. Céline gives no date; the patient gives May–June 1908. No doctor is named. The patient testified on 16 Mar 1911 (session 67); Céline in PO 4 f. 411v–412r; Marie des Anges on 9–10 Sep 1915 (PA 10 [905]).
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-193] Céline gives no date. Marie-Madeleine's own deposition (M-EW-194) dates the cure to May–June 1908 and does not mention a veil. Conflict on the veil. [EW-194] Céline (PO 4, f. 411v–412r) links the cure to a veil thought to have been Thérèse's; Marie-Madeleine does not mention any veil. See M-EW-193. [EW-203] M-EW-193 (Céline), M-EW-194 (the patient): about 30 boils here; 35 boils in the patient's count. Date: mid-May to June 1908.
- Source entries: M-EW-193, M-EW-194, M-EW-203
- Sources cited: [EW-193] PO 4, f. 412r, S34, W1 [EW-194] PO 18, f. 1109v, W1; the editors' note also mentions the cure; rights: facts-only [EW-203] PA 10, [905], session 45, W2

### M-151 — The Eure family (2): a boy of eleven with a fractured skull (22 August 1908)
- Date: 22 Aug 1908
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel (via Taylor or Laveille) (Taylor 1924 PDF 401–402; "A Priest" transmitting the sister's letter, March 1909)
- What was reported: The writer's brother, 11, fell about twenty feet through a trap door into a cellar on 22 Aug 1908. The skull was fractured in several places. A doctor gave him two hours and a surgeon agreed. A novena began on 24 Aug (the Carmel's prioress joining). He was unconscious and delirious eight days, with four crises. On the ninth day he recognized everyone and was calm, and he returned to school with no trace.
- Note: From M-OM-016. Medical facts in the source: doctor and surgeon consulted; no treatment named. Related: M-148 is the sister's own cure.
- Source entries: M-OM-016 (partial use: M-OM-016)

### M-152 — New York: a nun unhurt under a streetcar, 2 Sep 1908
- Date: 2 Sep 1908
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel (via Taylor or Laveille)
- What was reported: An exiled French nun crossing a crowded New York street was struck and thrown down by an electric car; she got up without a scratch (confirmed by the convent doctor); the conductor said she had fallen exactly between the wheels. She carried a small photograph of Thérèse.
- Witnesses and reporter: "Sister M.", letter 11 Jan 1909, countersigned by her prioress
- Status in the sources: recorded by the Carmel
- Source entries: M-OM-018
- Sources cited: Taylor 1924 PDF 388–389

### M-153 — The London Good Shepherd convent: the "prodigious fact" of 30 October 1908 and Miss Clark's foot (8 November 1908)
- Date: 30 Oct 1908
- Status label: sworn at the Church process; recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- Flag: possible duplicate: the deposition (M-EW-051) does not say what the event was; identity with Miss Clark's cure is likely but not proved, and the dates differ (30 Oct and 8 Nov 1908)
- What was reported: Fr Taylor testified that an extraordinary event happened at the Good Shepherd convent in London on 30 Oct 1908 which he publicized, and after which Thérèse's fame grew fast, especially in Scotland. Taylor 1924/1926 describes the cure of a badly hurt foot of a young postulant, Miss Clark (later a professed Dominican nun in London), on 8 Nov 1908; Taylor says Thérèse "kept her promise of 'coming down to earth'". The case was widely publicized, and in Glasgow (the girl's city) 450 favours were acknowledged in the Catholic press within a year.
- The person helped: [EW-051] not stated [OM-019] Miss Clark (named in Taylor 1924 note), later a professed Dominican nun in London
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-051] Taylor
- Status in the sources: [EW-051] mentioned in sworn testimony; no details [OM-019] recorded by the Carmel
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-051] Nature of the event not given in this deposition.
- Source entries: M-EW-051, M-OM-019
- Sources cited: [EW-051] PO-02, f. 182v–183r, session 10, 20 August 1910 [OM-019] Taylor 1926/30 PDF 422; Taylor 1924 PDF 422 note

### M-154 — Céline and the perfume of roses in Thérèse's cell (Nov 1908 onward)
- Date: Nov 1908
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: Céline, at first opposed to the idea of perfumes, found the oratory before Thérèse's former cell filled with a scent of roses in late Nov 1908, and felt a breath of it on the stairs. She said it happened about fifteen times in two years, never in the eleven years after Thérèse's death, never when she wished.
- The person helped: Céline
- Witnesses and reporter: Céline, alone
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process; not an approved miracle
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): She stayed unbelieving at first and did not look for a natural source (she says no scented letter was there). See M-EW-023, M-EW-024.
- Source entries: M-EW-191
- Sources cited: PO 4, f. 410v–411r, S34, W1

### M-156 — Pichon's leg ulcer healed, January 1909
- Date: 2 Jan 1909
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: After a leg fracture, a permanent congestion lasted nearly three years. Doctors warned a wound there would not heal. In 1908 a wound came, with heavy suppuration; for seven weeks several doctors failed to stop a deep ulcer several centimetres deep. On 2 Jan 1909 the doctor put him to bed without hope. He prayed to Thérèse; on the fifth day the wound was fully closed, and he could say Mass again. The doctor had ordered only bed rest.
- The person helped: Fr Almire Pichon SJ
- Witnesses and reporter: Pichon himself
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process; not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): fracture; chronic congestion; suppurating ulcer
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): not mentioned in PA-03, where he speaks of "two cures" but details only the pneumonia
- Source entries: M-EW-061
- Sources cited: PO-10 [551v–552r]

### M-159 — Pichon's broncho-pneumonia, Paris, August 1909
- Date: 28 Aug 1909
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: On 28 Aug 1909, during a retreat for the Augustinian nuns, rue de la Santé, Paris, he collapsed with infectious broncho-pneumonia following intestinal blockage. Three doctors said he would die within hours; he received Extreme Unction. He asked Thérèse to be "obedient in heaven as on earth" and to obtain a simple, quick cure "without too striking a miracle". The doctor said on day two, "there is a hidden saint behind this"; Pichon answered, "No, a woman saint." He said Mass on the 6th or 7th day (1911) or 4 days later (1915).
- The person helped: Fr Pichon
- Witnesses and reporter: Pichon; the nursing nuns prayed with him
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at both processes; not an approved miracle
- Words quoted: « médicalement, pour nous vous étiez mort » "medically, for us you were dead" (the doctor)
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): fever above 40 °C returned to normal the same day (1915 version); purulent broncho-pneumonia
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): the 1911 and 1915 accounts differ on the number of days before Mass (6–7 vs 4)
- Source entries: M-EW-069
- Sources cited: PO-10 [552r–v]; PA-03 [275] (clinic of the Augustinians, 29 rue de la Santé)

### M-160 — Water springs found for the Carmel of Oléron, autumn 1909
- Date: Oct 1909
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: The nuns asked her for water; a lady brought 100 francs; the water-finder priest, delayed in Austria, saw at night "a bright, majestic figure" (recognized from the picture sent him) who said, "You are forgetting, Father, the Carmelites of Oléron, who need you"; he came and found the springs.
- Witnesses and reporter: the prioress, 4 May 1910
- Status in the sources: recorded by the Carmel
- Source entries: M-OM-042
- Sources cited: Taylor 1924 PDF 383–384

### M-161 — Scent from the Life at the Abbaye aux Bois, Paris (c. 1908–1910)
- Date: c. 1909
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: The former novice mistress of the Abbaye aux Bois (Congregation of Notre-Dame, Paris) told Isabelle that the volume of Histoire d'une âme spread an extremely strong perfume through the monastery. It later weakened and stayed in the first engraving of Thérèse as a Carmelite, which stayed scented for nearly two years. A sister with her said the whole community smelled it. Natural explanations were sought and not found.
- The person helped: (a sign; no beneficiary)
- Witnesses and reporter: the visitor and her companion; Isabelle in the parlour
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process (second-hand)
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): Isabelle adds that almost every nun at Lisieux had at some time perceived such perfumes, and she herself several times, when she was not thinking of it. She says they usually come with an inner grace toward fervour. See M-EW-023, M-EW-191.
- Source entries: M-EW-202
- Sources cited: PO 16, [648v]–[649r], W1

### M-162 — Presezzi baby cured of double pneumonia (Trezzo d'Adda, Carnival 1910)
- Date: Feb 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: The only daughter of Ange Presezzi, about 18 months old, was dying of double pneumonia. The uncle, Carlino, came weeping to the Concesa church to ask prayers to the Virgin. Br Romée, the cook, said to pray to Thérèse instead ("she will do us the grace"). The lay brothers said 7 Paters and 7 Aves. Soon Carlino came back joyful: the child was cured. The next day she fell ill again; within fifteen days she was cured for good and still well in October 1910.
- The person helped: infant daughter of Ange Presezzi, Trezzo
- Witnesses and reporter: Br Romée (cook) and the lay brothers; Élie checked on 10 October 1910 with the widow Presezzi and her daughter Thérèse (about 22)
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process; not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): double pneumonia; fever 41° "et 3 lignes"; the father had already lost another girl of the same age to the same illness; relapse the next day; full cure in 15 days; the Trezzo doctor called it a miracle
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): The family attributed the cure to the Virgin Mary, not to Thérèse. The friars attributed it to Thérèse. Trezzo is about 15 minutes from Concesa.
- Source entries: M-EW-076
- Sources cited: PO-05, f. 447r–448r, session 38

### M-163 — The kitchen kettle that filled itself (Sr Jeanne-Marie, Lisieux Carmel, 1910)
- Date: 23 Feb 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process (first-hand by Sr Jeanne-Marie and Sr Marie-Madeleine; hearsay by Pauline, Marie, Céline and Isabelle); not an approved miracle
- What was reported: Sr Jeanne-Marie de l'Enfant-Jésus, a lay sister devoted to Thérèse, was overloaded with work and exhausted. She wondered what she would do if asked for more (Céline's version: she was on a ladder cleaning windows and at first thought of leaving the task). At that moment the cook, Sr Marie-Madeleine, asked her to empty, clean and refill the kitchen kettle (the stove's water tank; 65 litres, holding four 16-litre jugs, or four and a half jugs). She prayed to Thérèse and obeyed. They emptied and wiped it. Sr Marie-Madeleine filled a first jug at the pump next door; Sr Jeanne-Marie poured it in and went back for a second. When she returned to pour it, the kettle was full (Isabelle: as she began pouring the second jug; Céline: when she had poured barely a quarter; Marie: one jug of four). She called Sr Marie-Madeleine, who saw it and confirmed she had not filled it. Mère Agnès, not present, reports it as "a strange fact".
- The person helped: [EW-072, EW-104, EW-106] Sr Jeanne-Marie de l'Enfant-Jésus [EW-197] Sr Marie-Madeleine and Sr Jeanne-Marie
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-072] Sr Jeanne-Marie; Sr Marie-Madeleine; reported by Mère Agnès (not present) [EW-104] Marie (hearsay); Céline also reports it (C-M11) [EW-106] Marie, hearsay [EW-197] Isabelle (second-hand); Marie-Madeleine (first-hand, PO 18)
- Status in the sources: [EW-072, EW-106] sworn testimony (hearsay); not an approved miracle [EW-104] sworn testimony, hearsay [EW-197] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process; not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-072] kettle 65 L; jug 16 L
- Where the sources differ: Date: 23 Feb 1910 (Sr Jeanne-Marie, M-EW-014), "about Lent 1910" (Isabelle, M-EW-197); Marie and Céline give no date. Size of tank: "65 L; jug 16 L" (M-EW-072) and "four and a half jugs" (M-EW-197). How much was poured before the tank was full differs between the witnesses. The kettle had been emptied and cleaned just before. Isabelle's version differs from Marie-Madeleine's in details (F-EW-2507).
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-072] Pauline did not see it; she reports it as "a strange fact". [EW-104] Céline: filled "when she had poured barely a quarter". Consistent with Marie's "one jug of four". [EW-106] Date not given. [EW-197] M-EW-014, M-EW-072, M-EW-104, M-EW-106.
- Source entries: M-EW-072, M-EW-104, M-EW-106, M-EW-197, M-EW-014 (partial use: M-EW-014)
- Sources cited: [EW-072] PO 1, f. 298v–299r, Session 20, 3 Sept 1910 [EW-104] PO 3, f. 331r, S26 [EW-106] PA 7, [622], Session 26 [EW-197] PO 16, [646v]–[647r], W1

### M-164 — Charlotte, a penitent in a Refuge, March 1910
- Date: Mar 1910
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel (via Taylor or Laveille)
- What was reported: A penitent dying of influenza (black lips, doctor without hope) promised to stay in the Refuge if cured; a novena and relic followed; half an hour later she felt better and woke cured. When she again wanted to leave, the illness returned; she stayed, said she saw Sœur Thérèse at her side, and died on 26 Sep 1910.
- Witnesses and reporter: Charlotte's own letter (March 1910); letters of the Carmel of N. (April 1910) and of the Refuge superior (3 Jan 1911)
- Status in the sources: recorded by the Carmel
- Source entries: M-OM-025
- Sources cited: Taylor 1924 PDF 439–440

### M-165 — A dying man of 35: apparition and conversion, March 1910
- Date: 19 Mar 1910
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel (via Taylor or Laveille)
- What was reported: A man ill seven months and without religion since 20; a novena began 15 Mar with a relic; on 19 Mar, in coma with the death rattle, he sat up: "It is she! Yes, it is she! … I am cured!", confessed and received Communion; a fortnight later he relapsed and died after six weeks, having said he saw the Holy Face; seven family members and his valet returned to the sacraments.
- Witnesses and reporter: "Sister N.", September 1910
- Status in the sources: recorded by the Carmel
- Source entries: M-OM-026
- Sources cited: Taylor 1924 PDF 440–442

### M-166 — "Marie", eleven, Loire-Inférieure: walking again, Easter week 1910
- Date: 31 Mar 1910
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel (via Taylor or Laveille)
- What was reported: A frail pupil fell ill on 2 Jan 1910; her legs failed; three doctors treated weakness, then hip-joint disease; a specialist diagnosed paralysis of the spinal cord (a hump forming) and tried electric treatment twice, saying she might walk in a year. In the night between Wednesday and Thursday of Easter week she saw "a pretty little face" smiling: "You will soon be able to walk … this very day even!" At 3 pm a voice said "Walk!", and she ran to her mother. Three weeks later she made her First Communion; shown the book, she recognized the face.
- Witnesses and reporter: the head mistress, 20 Jul 1910
- Status in the sources: recorded by the Carmel
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): tonic, massage, treatment for hip disease, electricity.
- Source entries: M-OM-024
- Sources cited: Taylor 1924 PDF 403–405

### M-167 — Teresita Morali: orders reversed after touching a hair relic (Concesa, 1910)
- Date: 30 Jun 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: Élie gave Teresita Morali an envelope with Thérèse's hair. She was distressed at having to do something painful. She prayed, holding the relic to her heart. Within minutes she was called and given the opposite orders, exactly as she wished.
- The person helped: Teresita Morali, about 45, director of the Crespi asylum and prioress of the Carmelite secular tertiaries at Concesa
- Witnesses and reporter: her letter (dated 30-6-10) shown to the tribunal by Élie
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony with a letter; a favour, not a cure
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): Élie says the meeting was mid-July, but the letter is dated 30 June 1910; one date is wrong.
- Source entries: M-EW-075
- Sources cited: PO-05, f. 446v–447r, session 38

### M-168 — "Gallipoli": a voice tells a French Carmelite "You must make me known everywhere", 20 July 1910
- Date: 20 Jul 1910
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel (via Taylor or Laveille); connected with the Gallipoli series M-006
- What was reported: A French Carmelite, writing to an exiled nun in Belgium about Gallipoli, forgot the town's name; she asked Thérèse and heard a voice say "Gallipoli", then: "That is just what you must do. You must make me known everywhere. I desire to do so much good." She felt her presence without seeing her.
- Status in the sources: recorded by the Carmel
- Source entries: M-OM-012
- Sources cited: Taylor 1924 PDF 382; Taylor 1926/30 PDF 438

### M-169 — Sr Marie de l'Enfant-Jésus, cured of a returning pneumonia, Lisieux, 29 July 1910
- Date: 29 Jul 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: A novice, Sr Marie de l'Enfant-Jésus, clothed 16 July 1910, had been sent away once after two years' postulancy for poor health. She felt a pneumonia return that had made her gravely ill the year before. She had pain in her back, side and chest, could hardly breathe, had fever and general malaise. Night of 28–29 July she coughed and did not sleep. On 29 July the prioress sent her to rest. In her cell, discouraged, she begged Mère Marie-Ange to come and heal her with Sister Thérèse. She fell into "a kind of supernatural sleep" and felt the Servant of God and her companion near, so clearly that she thought, "If I open my eyes I will see them", but did not dare. She felt a refreshing dew tempering the fever, and the back pain left. Then the side and chest pains left as she prayed. Her soul was flooded with light and delight, and she felt the Servant of God showed her all her faults. As a sign she asked not to cough for the rest of the day, and she did not. The light continued for several days.
- The person helped: Sr Marie de l'Enfant-Jésus, novice
- Witnesses and reporter: Isabelle heard it from the novice; the novice herself was not one of these witnesses.
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process (second-hand); not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): pneumonia the year before; pain in back, side and chest; fever; cough; no doctor's report.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): Isabelle says she believes Thérèse intervened to prevent the novice's dismissal, because the novice is "a child of sound judgment", her behaviour changed, and her usual dryness of soul went. No doctor is named.
- Source entries: M-EW-195
- Sources cited: PO 16, [645v]–[646v], W1

### M-170 — Violet perfume at the first exhumation, 6 September 1910
- Date: 6 Sep 1910
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel (via Taylor or Laveille)
- What was reported: When the coffin was opened on 6 Sep 1910, two workmen (unnamed) said they smelled "a sweet and strong violet perfume". Laveille and Taylor report that the bones "exhaled a sweet perfume", noted by several witnesses. Only bones remained after thirteen years, as she had foretold. The palm was "green and fresh" (F-OM-028).
- The person helped: [OM-101] none (a sign, not a healing)
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-101] two workmen (not named); account in *Pluie de roses* 1911 pp. 103–109, on the Carmel site
- Status in the sources: [OM-101] recorded by the Carmel
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [OM-101] a coffin left 13 years in wet ground; the palm "green and fresh" is the same report as F-OM-028.
- Where the sources differ: The flower (violet) is named only in the Carmel account (Pluie de roses 1911 pp. 103–109). Laveille does not discuss a natural cause. The coffin had lain 13 years in wet ground.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [OM-101] the flower is named only in the Carmel account.
- Source entries: M-OM-101, M-LF-013 (partial use: M-LF-013)
- Sources cited: [OM-101] Carmel /les-exhumations-de-therese/a-lisieux-les-recits-anciens-des-deux-exhumations/; F-OM-028; F-OM-113. Rights: unknown.

### M-171 — A young man of 28 at the sanatorium of La Pouverine, 1910
- Date: 8 Sep 1910
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel (via Taylor or Laveille)
- What was reported: A consumptive of bitter character, 13 years away from religion, converted after a prayer to her for conversion "on a fixed date" and received Communion on 8 Sep 1910; he died devoutly.
- Witnesses and reporter: "Auguste", La Pouverine near Cuers, 10 Jul 1911
- Status in the sources: recorded by the Carmel
- Source entries: M-OM-027
- Sources cited: Taylor 1924 PDF 442–443

### M-172 — Incense smell from a coffin plank, Lisieux Carmel (September 1910)
- Date: 15 Sep 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process (second-hand for the smell); not an approved miracle
- What was reported: A plank of the coffin was identified, right after the exhumation, by an incense smell that several sisters noticed; Céline did not. In Céline's account of 15 Sep 1910, Marie de la Trinité, Sr Marie de l'Enfant-Jésus and Sr Thérèse de l'Eucharistie smelled a strong odour of incense from a board brought in from the cemetery brushwood; the prioress and Céline smelled nothing. Céline compared the board with the real coffin boards and found it identical. A workman had hidden it in the brushwood himself in order to fetch it later. Dr La Néele recognised the board.
- The person helped: [EW-192] (no beneficiary; a sign)
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-192] Marie de la Trinité and two others (smelled it); Céline (reporter, did not smell it)
- Status in the sources: [EW-192] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process (second-hand for the smell); not an approved miracle
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-192] See M-EW-074. Céline notes the workman's hiding of the board.
- Note: M-EW-074 says the plank was identified "right after the exhumation" (6 Sep 1910); Céline's account of the board is dated 15 Sep 1910. No medical or natural facts beyond the workman's hiding of the board.
- Source entries: M-EW-192, M-EW-074 (partial use: M-EW-074)
- Sources cited: [EW-192] PO 4, f. 411r–v, S34, W1

### M-173 — A Canadian boy of six cured of a leg sore, and his wish to be buried with "his little saint" (1910)
- Date: Oct 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: A Canadian doctor's wife came from Paris with her son (6½) to visit the grave. At age 4 he had been tuberculous with a foot sore so bad that amputation was planned (the family credited St Anne with that cure). Two and a half years later a new sore formed on his leg and worsened for three weeks. He made a novena to "his little saint" and at the end was completely cured. At the grave he did not want to leave. He told a man there he wanted to be buried with his little saint; when his mother said he would not be brought back from Canada, he said he would be buried with her picture in his hands, "and it will be the same". He wrote his name on the grave's cross and asked to make a good first communion so he could become a priest.
- The person helped: [EW-077] an unnamed Canadian boy, 6½ [EW-124] the Canadian boy
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-077] the mother, who told Élie in the Carmel parlour on 25 October 1910; Élie SAW the boy [EW-124] Mère Agnès, in a letter to Élie, from the mother's account
- Status in the sources: [EW-077] sworn testimony (hearsay from the mother) [EW-124] letter quoted in sworn testimony
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-077] tuberculosis to age 4; foot sore; amputation considered; second leg sore worsening for three weeks
- Where the sources differ: The mother told Élie in the Carmel parlour on 25 Oct 1910 (Élie saw the boy; hearsay from the mother). M-EW-124 is Mère Agnès's letter to Élie from the mother's account; Élie calls this a "spiritual miracle". No medical certificate is mentioned.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-077] No medical certificate mentioned. [EW-124] Élie calls this a "spiritual miracle".
- Source entries: M-EW-077, M-EW-124
- Sources cited: [EW-077] PO-05, f. 448r–449r, session 38 [EW-124] PO-05, f. 448v–449r

### M-174 — M. Brossard of Passy accepts the last sacraments (Paris, 1910)
- Date: Oct 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: M. Brossard, a man of private means, rue de la Pompe, was gravely ill and stubbornly refused the priest. A novena to Thérèse was begun. Before its end he gladly received the priest, confessed, and received Extreme Unction fully conscious.
- The person helped: M. Brossard, rue de la Pompe 15 (or 17), Paris (Passy)
- Witnesses and reporter: Fr Ferdinand Fabre OCD, letter of 25 October 1910 to the Lisieux prioress, handed to the tribunal
- Status in the sources: letter in sworn testimony; spiritual grace
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): grave illness, no diagnosis
- Source entries: M-EW-078
- Sources cited: PO-05, f. 449v–450r

### M-175 — Fr Ernest, novice master of Leffe, cured of laryngitis (October 1910)
- Date: Oct 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process (Abbot Godefroy); called a "very special favour"
- What was reported: Fr Ernest had stubborn laryngitis that made chanting, teaching and even conversation painful, and for some weeks impossible. Remedies advised by several doctors failed. During a novena to Thérèse he was completely cured; the cure lasted from October 1910. He now works as novice master, cantor and teacher without fatigue.
- The person helped: Fr Ernest, O.Praem., novice master, Leffe
- Witnesses and reporter: Godefroy
- Status in the sources: "very special favour"
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): tenacious laryngitis; voice loss for weeks; several doctors' remedies failed
- Source entries: M-EW-083
- Sources cited: PO-24, f. 1222r–1222v

### M-176 — Br Vincent de Paul cured of neurasthenia on a set day (Leffe, 14 October 1910)
- Date: 14 Oct 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process (Abbot Godefroy); called a "very special favour", not a miracle
- What was reported: A young canon of Godefroy's community, about 25, suffered from deep, persistent neurasthenia for several years. Two months of hydrotherapy did almost nothing. He made a novena and asked for a cure on a fixed day, 14 October 1910. That day he was able to preach, and since then studies and preaches without fatigue.
- The person helped: Br Vincent de Paul, O.Praem., about 25, Leffe abbey
- Witnesses and reporter: Abbot Godefroy
- Status in the sources: Godefroy calls it "a very special favour", not a miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): several years of neurasthenia; two months of hydrotherapy without result
- Source entries: M-EW-085
- Sources cited: PO-24, f. 1222r, session 78, 24 May 1911

### M-177 — Sandalwood perfume from Thérèse's pen and pencil (Lisieux, 24 October 1910)
- Date: 24 Oct 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: When Élie kissed Thérèse's pen and pencil, he cried out "Oh, how good it smells!" He smelled a sweet scent like sandalwood, well known to him from India. He smelled it again on some other objects, but not on her mantle or veil. The nuns present smelled nothing but mustiness. They said nothing had been put in the boxes or cloths to cause a smell, and believed it miraculous.
- The person helped: Fr Élie
- Witnesses and reporter: Fr Élie; the nuns present (unnamed)
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony; Élie says he reported it only because he was told it would be a scruple to leave it out
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): the nuns smelled only mould
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): Élie says it might be his "fantaisie" or a trick of the devil to make him vain. The judge asked if he had heard of such perfumes before; he said a letter a year earlier had mentioned them, but he was not thinking of it.
- Source entries: M-EW-086
- Sources cited: PO-05, f. 452v–453v, session 39, 31 October 1910

### M-178 — The box of cut relic "authentics" that filled itself (Sr Jeanne-Marie, Lisieux Carmel, 1910)
- Date: Dec 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process (Sr Jeanne-Marie herself, and Isabelle second-hand); not an approved miracle
- What was reported: Sr Jeanne-Marie was preparing small Thérèse souvenirs (relics), to each of which she had to stick the vice-postulator's authentication slip. In Christmas week 1910, preparing 900–1,000 relic "authentiques" for the prioress's feast with only about 100 cut, she saw the box of slips nearly empty and the sisters who would cut them busy. As usual she asked Sister Thérèse to help. After a short absence she came back to her cell (at 3 p.m., M-EW-014) and found the box completely full of cut slips. No sister had been in (she asked the whole community). The prioress believed her.
- The person helped: [EW-198] Sr Jeanne-Marie
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-198] Isabelle reports; Jeanne-Marie asked the whole community and no one had entered
- Status in the sources: [EW-198] sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process (second-hand); not an approved miracle
- Where the sources differ: M-EW-198 (Isabelle, second-hand) says "1910" without a date and notes "check against M-EW-014". M-EW-014 puts it in Christmas week 1910.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-198] Same year 1910. Check against M-EW-014.
- Source entries: M-EW-198, M-EW-014 (partial use: M-EW-014)
- Sources cited: [EW-198] PO 16, [647r]–[647v], W1

### M-179 — White Fathers' mission at Tabora (Tanganyika): crowds come after catechists receive her picture, December 1910
- Date: Dec 1910
- Status label: letter quoted in sworn testimony (Marie, PA 7 [622]–[624]) and recorded by the Carmel; not a miracle claim
- What was reported: Fr A. Van Aken, White Father at Tabora, put her picture in huts and catechism halls. After his catechists prayed to her for the country's conversion, pagans came "in compact crowds" ("in whole crowds"), including from unvisited, formerly hostile villages. "Pessimists" doubted it would last.
- Note: Sources cited: Laveille PDF 408–409 (Summarium 1919 p. 1022 §2504); PA 7 [622]–[624], Session 26. Van Aken himself records doubters (M-EW-073). Laveille (M-LF-041) also mentions a White Father in Africa who reported many conversions after her picture was placed in villages.
- Source entries: M-OM-057, M-EW-073, M-LF-041 (partial use: M-EW-073, M-LF-041)

### M-180 — Greenock: the Little Sisters of the Poor's rates, December 1910
- Date: Dec 1910
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel (via Taylor or Laveille)
- What was reported: The superioress owed £68 in rates; after prayer, £3 came from a benefactor; on the due day she paid £25 and asked for time; that afternoon a legacy of £65 arrived.
- Status in the sources: recorded by the Carmel
- Source entries: M-OM-028
- Sources cited: Taylor 1924 PDF 387

### M-181 — Mme Poirier (Berthe Chopin), near Ambrières
- Date: c. 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: A woman ill for two years with a serious internal illness, called hopeless by doctors several times, invoked Thérèse with a cloth relic. Recently she arrived unexpectedly in Lisieux, though before she could hardly walk between rooms, to pray at the tomb in thanks.
- The person helped: Mme Poirier, née Berthe Chopin, near Ambrières (diocese of Laval), niece of Marcelline's brother
- Witnesses and reporter: Marcelline's brother (letter); Mlle Aimée Roger of Lisieux (eyewitness)
- Status in the sources: hearsay in sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process; not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): illness unnamed; "hopeless" per doctors
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): nature of illness unknown to witness
- Source entries: M-EW-113
- Sources cited: PO-08 [523v–524r], Session 48, 15 Dec 1910

### M-182 — Mlle Louise Alexandre, eye disease, cured at the tomb
- Date: c. 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: a serious eye disease, seen and treated by a doctor, cured suddenly and completely while she prayed at Thérèse's tomb
- The person helped: Mlle Louise Alexandre, about 50, of Saint-Front-de-Colliere
- Witnesses and reporter: herself to Dumaine; he signed her report to the prioress as to her honesty
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process; not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): eye disease diagnosed by a doctor
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): no medical record cited
- Source entries: M-EW-114
- Sources cited: PO-06 [464r]

### M-183 — Knee cured with earth from the grave
- Date: c. 1910
- Status label: anonymous note found at the tomb (Valadier, Ordinary Process); not a miracle claim in law
- What was reported: a girl wrote on a paper left at the tomb that her knee was healed by a poultice of earth from the grave
- The person helped: an unnamed girl
- Witnesses and reporter: Valadier found the note
- Status in the sources: anonymous note; not a miracle claim in law
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): knee complaint
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): anonymous; also shows the practice of taking grave earth
- Source entries: M-EW-117
- Sources cited: PO-11 [559r]

### M-184 — Three deathbed conversions in Maupas's parish (Lisieux, c. 1910)
- Date: c. 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: Maupas commended to Thérèse three dying people whose conversion was difficult and credits her with the unexpected conversion of three dying men. One, involved in the seizure of Church property in France and under Church censures, refused to retract and would need a public retraction to get a Church burial. Maupas made a novena asking, as a sign, that the man send for him himself. Near its end he did: he sent his nurse to fetch Maupas, retracted, received all the sacraments and died a Christian (edifyingly).
- The person helped: [EW-118] three unnamed dying men [EW-138] an unnamed man involved in confiscation of Church goods; two others
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-118] Maupas [EW-138] Maupas, first-hand
- Status in the sources: [EW-118] sworn testimony at the Apostolic Process; spiritual graces [EW-138] sworn testimony
- Where the sources differ: Two depositions of Maupas: Apostolic Process PA-15 [1004] and Ordinary Process PO-25 f. 1231v–1233r (the public copy skips f. 1232). No names, dates or places.
- Source entries: M-EW-118, M-EW-138
- Sources cited: [EW-118] PA-15 [1004] [EW-138] PO-25, f. 1231v–1233r (the public copy skips f. 1232)

### M-185 — Dream of an old man in a Little Sisters' home
- Date: c. 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: An old man was given a picture of Thérèse to reconcile him to death. In a dream he saw everything ready for his burial. Thérèse appeared, raised her hand, and said the time of death had not yet come. His fervour grew. The eve of his death he asked the Little Sister: "Don't you see that beautiful lady?" He died like a saint.
- The person helped: an unnamed old man
- Witnesses and reporter: a letter to Taylor
- Status in the sources: letter reported in sworn testimony
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): The "beautiful lady" is linked by the writer to his devotion to Mary.
- Source entries: M-EW-121
- Sources cited: PO-02, f. 203r–203v

### M-186 — Worker cured of religious madness after a novena
- Date: c. 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: A poor worker had "religious madness" for a year. Several doctors said it was a very difficult case. At the end of a novena to Thérèse he was much better, and later fully cured.
- The person helped: an unnamed worker
- Witnesses and reporter: letter to Taylor
- Status in the sources: letter quoted in sworn testimony
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): one year of illness; several doctors; no improvement before the novena
- Source entries: M-EW-122
- Sources cited: PO-02, f. 203v

### M-187 — Sisters of Mercy superior's difficulty resolved
- Date: c. 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: A superior in serious difficulty promised to hang Thérèse's portrait; matters settled a few days later.
- The person helped: superior of the Sisters of Mercy, Glasgow
- Witnesses and reporter: Taylor
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony, hearsay
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): The difficulty is not described.
- Source entries: M-EW-123
- Sources cited: PO-02, f. 205r

### M-188 — Sr Jean-Baptiste's petrol lamp burns for a full hour (Lisieux Carmel, c. 1910)
- Date: c. 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: Sr Saint Jean-Baptiste, about 60, was making small sachets for Thérèse relics during the time of great silence. Her petrol lamp began to smoke and go out; she had forgotten to fill it. The rule forbade filling the lamps in the evening because of the danger of explosion. She called on Sister Thérèse. At once the flame revived and burned steadily for the whole hour.
- The person helped: Sr Saint Jean-Baptiste
- Witnesses and reporter: Isabelle reports; no other witness named
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process (second-hand); not an approved miracle
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): Date not given. Check whether she is the Sr Saint-Jean-Baptiste of the batch K biography cards.
- Source entries: M-EW-196
- Sources cited: PO 16, [646v], W1

### M-189 — Mme Leroyer's rose and violet scents, Lisieux (c. 1910) — TSA's mother, via TSA
- Date: c. 1910
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: TSA said her mother (a widow, Mme Leroyer, who works in Lisieux in the catechism charity) had asked Thérèse about a year earlier to be her guardian angel. One day, entering a shop on business, the shopkeeper said to her spontaneously and knowing nothing of her devotion, "What a delicious smell of roses you bring with you!" She had nothing on her that could explain it. On another day a visitor at her home said she smelled violets, which had no natural cause. Mme Leroyer smelled nothing herself in either case. TSA also perceived lilac or violet twice, when nothing natural explained it and she was not expecting it. TSA said she does not want these sensible favours.
- The person helped: Mme Leroyer (widow), TSA's mother
- Witnesses and reporter: the shopkeeper and a visitor (unnamed); TSA reports her mother's account, and vouches for her mother's discreet, unenthusiastic character
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process (second-hand); not an approved miracle
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): TSA's circular (1929) adds that her mother felt Thérèse "always present at her side" and that TSA received a thorn on 2 Jan 1911 that foretold her mother's death on 27 Feb 1911 (F-EW-2555).
- Source entries: M-EW-204
- Sources cited: PO 13, [590r]–[591r], session 56, 15 Feb 1911, W1

### M-190 — Mgr Augouard (Vicar Apostolic of Ubangi) and a missionary with sleeping sickness, French Congo, c. 1910
- Date: c. 1910
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel (via Taylor or Laveille)
- What was reported: Mgr Prosper Augouard, Vicar Apostolic of Ubangi, was in bed for three months with painful rheumatism (Laveille: "for two years he had been confined by painful rheumatism for three months"). He read her Life and asked her for his cure or his death, then withdrew the petition in the night: "I ask nothing now for myself, but save Fr ... from death." The missionary had sleeping sickness. Sent to France, he was examined at the Pasteur Institute and "pronounced entirely free from any microbe", returned to the Congo healthy, and never again showed symptoms. The bishop himself "was cured the day after I had withdrawn my petition". He made a thanksgiving pilgrimage to her tomb and asked the pope to introduce the cause. Letter from Brazzaville, 10 Nov 1912 (Pluie de roses 1912, p. 107).
- The person helped: [LF-022] Mgr Augouard; an unnamed missionary
- Witnesses and reporter: [OM-023] Mgr Prosper Augouard, letter from Brazzaville, 10 Nov 1912 [LF-022] Mgr Augouard (letter printed in *Pluie de roses* 1912 p.107)
- Status in the sources: [OM-023] recorded by the Carmel [LF-022] recorded by the shrine or monastery
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [OM-023] Pasteur Institute examination of the missionary. [LF-022] rheumatism; sleeping sickness diagnosed; Pasteur Institute examination.
- Where the sources differ: Date: 1910–1912 in Laveille, about 1910 in Taylor 1924, and "She cured in 1909 Mgr. Augouard ... together with one of his missionaries" in Taylor 1926/30 (PDF 452–453). The missionary's name is withheld in the letter.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [LF-022] The missionary's name is withheld in the letter.
- Source entries: M-OM-023, M-LF-022
- Sources cited: [OM-023] Laveille PDF 411–412 (citing *Shower of Roses* for 1912, p. 107); Taylor 1926/30 PDF 452–453 ("She cured in 1909 Mgr. Augouard … together with one of his missionaries") — date conflict 1909/1910. [LF-022] LAV p.411–412

### M-191 — Santa Aprile of Nardò, cured of gangrenous erysipelas, night of 2–3 Jan 1911
- Date: 2 Jan 1911
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: Miss Santa Aprile, about 50, living opposite the cathedral of Nardò, fell ill on 1 Jan 1911 with phlegmonous erysipelas of the face and throat "of gangrenous character". In 48 hours she was at the point of death. The two doctors, Zuccalà and Tarantino, saw no hope of a crisis that would resolve it. Necrosis had begun in the mouth; the face was horribly swollen and they thought of a tracheotomy. She was conscious and ready to die, but she trusted she would be cured only by the intercession of Sister Thérèse. During the night, when all was ready for her burial, she was fully awake, her eyes shut by the swelling, and "saw mentally" a very white hand touch her face gently. She felt it was Sister Thérèse and felt a beginning of well-being. Next day the doctors found her better; the necrosis began to clear. She recovered fully, slowly, in spite of a new crisis of two or three days.
- The person helped: Santa Aprile, about 50, Nardò
- Witnesses and reporter: Giannattasio says he knew the family personally and had it from the patient, her mother, her two brothers and her sister.
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Ordinary Process (second-hand); not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): phlegmonous erysipelas with gangrene of face and throat, onset 1 Jan; necrosis in the mouth; possible tracheotomy; recovery slow, with a relapse of two or three days; doctors Zuccalà and Tarantino.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): The doctors did not testify in the material we have. No medical certificate is given in the deposition.
- Source entries: M-EW-145
- Sources cited: PO 33, [1328r]–[1329r], W1

### M-192 — Sr Alexandra, Norbertine novice, cured of enteritis (Grimbergen, 12 January 1911)
- Date: 12 Jan 1911
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: A Norbertine novice of Bonlieu abbey (diocese of Valence), exiled since 1901 at Grimbergen (Brabant), had enteritis for several years. It worsened: appetite and sleep vanished, inner pains were sharp, and her death was expected. Two doctors called the state grave but disagreed on cause and treatment. The abbess thought of sending her home. She wanted to keep her vocation and turned to Thérèse; during a community novena she was suddenly cured on 12 January 1911. She then kept the strict Lent of her order.
- The person helped: Sr Alexandra, Norbertine novice
- Witnesses and reporter: Godefroy; a written report signed by the abbess and officers, shown to the judges
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony with a document; not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): enteritis for years; loss of appetite and sleep; severe internal pain; two doctors disagreeing; sudden cure
- Source entries: M-EW-087
- Sources cited: PO-24, f. 1222v–1223r

- What was reported: A postulant of the Little Sisters of the Poor, Glasgow (entered 24 Sep 1910), lost the sight of her left eye after a fall; the community doctor diagnosed cataract (5 Jan 1911); the West Regent Ophthalmic Institution and the Glasgow Eye Infirmary declared it incurable; after a novena of Communions she found she could see on leaving church on 25 Feb 1911; readmitted to the convent.
- Witnesses and reporter: Dr David Jones, MB CM (certificate 19 Aug 1911: opacity of the vitreous humour, probably from childhood; "she can see perfectly well"); Rev. H. Grey Graham (30 Jun 1911)
- Status in the sources: recorded by the Carmel
- Source entries: M-OM-031
- Sources cited: Taylor 1924 PDF 407–409

### M-198 — Scent of orris root from the earth of the first grave (Carmel attic, March–April 1911)
- Date: 22 Mar 1911
- Status label: sworn at the Church process (Céline); recorded by the Carmel (Laveille); not an approved miracle
- What was reported: Earth taken from under the first coffin at the exhumation was brought to the Carmel in slag sacks and left in the attic, first mouldy then cracked. On Wednesday 22 March 1911 Céline smelled a delicious scent of orris root from it; again on 22 and 24 April; another sister smelled it too. The earth was then taken out, pounded by two men for several days, and kept with honour. Laveille: the earth that touched the coffin kept its fragrance for months.
- Medical or natural facts: mould and drying of the earth (M-EW-074).
- Where the sources differ: Céline admits she did nothing after the first scent.
- Source entries: M-EW-074, M-LF-013 (partial use: M-EW-074, M-LF-013)

### M-199 — Money from Glasgow after Grant's conversion (May 1911)
- Date: May 1911
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: His conversion cost Grant his livelihood. On a Thursday in May 1911, alone in his room in Edinburgh in the afternoon, he "saw" Thérèse standing by him looking toward Glasgow, and felt she was doing something for him there. He told his wife: "Thérèse is busy with us at this moment." The following Thursday, unexpected help came from Glasgow.
- The person helped: Grant and his wife
- Witnesses and reporter: Grant; his wife
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony
- Where the sources differ: In 1915 Grant says he felt her presence twice, and "the next day" a Glasgow priest, himself a converted ex-Protestant minister, brought unasked a cheque for £200, though they had hidden their need. In 1911 he said "the following Thursday". Related: M-036.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): CONFLICT: in 1915 he says he felt her presence twice, and "the next day" a Glasgow priest, himself a converted ex-Protestant minister, brought unasked a cheque for £200, though they had hidden their need. In 1911 he said "the following Thursday".
- Source entries: M-EW-091
- Sources cited: PO-27, f. 1255v–1256r; PA-05, p. 320–321, session 9, 1 June 1915

- What was reported: After a fall, painful foot; a doctor said surgery might be needed. She secretly placed a small relic on it; next day the bone was painless and she could walk; no relapse. Also a hard family affair resolved unexpectedly.

She wrote to Auriault that before the grave she suddenly understood that Thérèse reached her love through much hidden suffering, and she felt "illuminated by a new light" and a need to follow the way of suffering.

### M-214 — Sara Pecquet, Paris, 26 January 1912 (elephantiasis)
- Date: 26 Jan 1912
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel (via Taylor or Laveille)
- What was reported: A dressmaker of 62, cured at Lourdes in 1900 of elephantiasis and scrofula, offered in 1910 to take back her illness for her mother; the illness returned worse: both legs swollen, erysipelas, gangrene, hip disease and hernia; in June 1911 Paris doctors called the leg wounds incurable. On 26 Jan 1912 she applied a relic under the bandages; the pain followed the relic down the leg; at 5.30 pm she found the swelling gone and new skin; she and her mother watched the wound edges close; next day the nursing sister found the leg sound (circumference over 36 inches reduced to 10).
- Witnesses and reporter: Geneviève de Lalain Chomel, Paris, 9 Mar 1912, signed also by witnesses and the visiting priest
- Status in the sources: recorded by the Carmel (Taylor calls it "well-authenticated")
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): Paris doctors; bandages; the doctor said "It is quite impossible to understand."
- Source entries: M-OM-038
- Sources cited: Taylor 1924 PDF 411–414

- Date: 5 Feb 1912
- Status label: sworn at the Church process (Céline alone); personal; not an approved miracle
- What was reported: On 5 Feb 1912, anniversary of Céline's clothing and the day the diocesan process was lodged in Rome, she was woken at night by a very strong smell of syringa and heard something like a dove flutter and land on her pillow, but saw nothing.
- Note: From M-EW-093 (PA 8 [806], Session 39). Céline says such cases are rare: five or six in five years. No medical or natural facts mentioned. See also M-235.
- Source entries: M-EW-093 (partial use: M-EW-093)

On the 7th day of a novena, 20 May 1912, about 2 am, a voice said three times "Awake! Awake!"; the leg moved; in the morning she walked and jumped; foot straight, hip in place, leg of normal length.

- Witnesses and reporter: Grant SAW the eye

### M-222 — Mme Boulay's lip cyst (Alençon)
- Date: c. 1912
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: Mme Boulay, the Grants' next-door neighbour, had a large cyst on her lip that grew for three months. The doctor said an operation would be needed within ten days. She stopped medical treatment; with the Grants she made four novenas; she was completely cured with no trace.
- The person helped: Mme Boulay, rue Saint-Blaise, Alençon
- Witnesses and reporter: Grant, first-hand
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): large lip cyst growing for three months; operation planned in ten days; Grant's own impression was a cancerous cyst, because it looked like cancers he had known
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): He does not know the doctor's diagnosis.
- Source entries: M-EW-139
- Sources cited: PA-05, p. 310–311

- Witnesses and reporter: Grant
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): illness requiring operation, not named

### M-228 — Leffe abbey's bonds untouched by German soldiers (24 August 1914)
- Date: 24 Aug 1914
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: On 24 August 1914 German ("Prussian") troops seized all the monks of Leffe (Dinant) and jailed them in the regimental school barracks. The monks had hidden their few bond certificates deep in a cellar, with a relic of Thérèse in each packet. An officer made Fr Joseph lead him there, poked everything with his bayonet, moved the packets, and passed on. 1,500–1,800 civilian prisoners then held in the abbey went everywhere, even the cellars, but nobody touched the four very visible packets. A month later the abbey was found looted from top to bottom, but the bonds were intact.
- The person helped: the community of Leffe
- Witnesses and reporter: Godefroy
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Apostolic Process (1917)
- Source entries: M-EW-098
- Sources cited: PA-26, f. 39r–39v, session 3, 6 September 1917

- What was reported: Twenty-five Carmelites of Marche, condemned to be shot "on August 31" (1914), were pardoned at dawn.
- Source entries: M-OM-079 (partial use: M-OM-079)

### M-230 — The Leffe monks spared and freed at Marche (August–September 1914)
- Date: 24 Sep 1914
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: Abbot Godefroy Madelaine and the monks of the Abbey of Leffe each wore her relic and survived massacres at Dinant (23 Aug 1914). In the barracks they expected to be shot at any moment. They made a vow to Thérèse; after four days they were still alive. On the fifth day they were marched all day to Marche (about 30 km) and interned on parole in the Carmelite friary. After three weeks of captivity all the prisoners (religious, clergy, Christian Brothers) made a novena. On its last day (24 Sep 1914, the ninth day of a novena), through the Bishop of Namur's intervention, the provincial governor came with his staff to announce they were free. Separately, two English scholastics or novices of Leffe held at Dinant and Namur for deportation came back before a novena for them ended (released 28 Nov 1914).
- The person helped: [EW-097] the canons of Leffe; other religious prisoners; two English scholastics
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-097] Godefroy (a prisoner himself)
- Status in the sources: [EW-097] sworn testimony, 1917
- Where the sources differ: Taylor says the imprisonment was at Marche; Dinant was the site of a massacre of civilians on 23 Aug 1914 (context, not in the source).
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-097] Dinant was the site of a massacre of civilians on 23 August 1914 (context, not in the source).
- Source entries: M-EW-097, M-OM-079 (partial use: M-OM-079)
- Sources cited: [EW-097] PA-26, f. 39v–40r

### M-231 — André Pelletier, 30 September 1914, 7 p.m.
- Date: 30 Sep 1914
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: On 30 September 1914, anniversary of the death, Céline expected a sign to guide the troops and at 7 p.m. (the hour of the death) climbed to the attic to look at the horizon. The prioress pitied her ("Poor little thing, how can she hope for that!"). She saw nothing. In June 1915 Abbé Charles, parish priest of Bagnolet (Seine), reported that soldier André Pelletier, 43rd Colonial Infantry, had seen Thérèse on 30 September at 7 p.m., as they attacked a wood, urging them forward. He alone saw her; he looked several times thinking it a hallucination. The position was taken against expectation, and the soldier, far from God, was converted.
- The person helped: André Pelletier and his unit
- Witnesses and reporter: Pelletier (via his parish priest); Céline reports it
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony (hearsay); not approved
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): The soldier himself suspected hallucination at first.
- Source entries: M-EW-099
- Sources cited: PA 8, [808]–[809], Session 39

### M-232 — Soldier Claude Cholet, lung wound, 1914
- Date: c. 1914
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: A bullet entered his back and left through the upper chest, perforating the lung. Fever, choking, suppuration; one night he was thought near death and given Viaticum. The army doctor said the danger was serious. After a novena to Thérèse he improved steadily and was convalescent at home.
- The person helped: Claude Prosper Arthur Cholet, 60 rue Vivy, Cours (Rhône)
- Witnesses and reporter: Dumaine, chaplain, SAW
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Apostolic Process; not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): perforated lung, fever, suppuration
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): gradual recovery; natural course possible
- Source entries: M-EW-095
- Sources cited: PA-02 [250–251]

### M-233 — Adjutant Ernest Cholet, thigh wound, amputation feared
- Date: c. 1914
- Status label: sworn at the Church process
- What was reported: A bullet crossed his thigh, involving the femur. The wound was bad and painful; the surgeon expected amputation. After a novena the wound improved and was healing.

- Witnesses and reporter: Dumaine
- Status in the sources: sworn testimony at the Apostolic Process; not an approved miracle
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): femur involved; amputation considered
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): "on the way to healing" only
- Source entries: M-EW-115
- Sources cited: PA-02 [251]

- What was reported: Marcelline had varicose ulcers on the leg at various times. In Dec 1916, after unusual fatigue, a violent inflammation began, which from experience would become an ulcer. No one could replace her in her work. She put a relic of Thérèse on the leg; without remedy or rest, the inflammation vanished.

### M-249 — Laura Fitchett, Dublin, 27 Jan 1921

- Status label: investigated privately by Prof. Patrick Beecher of Maynooth (16 Apr 1925), not by a Church tribunal; attestations printed in the Shower of Roses
- What was reported: Girl of 13 with acute nephritis and dropsy (Dr Garland: no hope), anointed; saw Thérèse (as a child in First Communion dress, curls, white silk bag) with the Virgin; told to say three Hail Marys; given a white rose to smell; "Good-bye now, Laura, you are cured." She laid her on the earth of the open grave above the coffin and prayed. After a few moments the child sat up and walked unaided from the tomb.
- The person helped: [LF-030] a child (god-daughter), unnamed
- Witnesses and reporter: [LF-030] Laveille (no note)
- Status in the sources: [LF-030] recorded by the shrine

- Where the sources differ: The Carmel (via M-OM-080) says a 12-year-old with Pott's disease walked. Laveille: "half-paralysed". No doctor named.
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [LF-030] "half-paralysed, unable to walk for months"; no doctor.
- Source entries: M-LF-030, M-OM-080
- Sources cited: [LF-030] LAV p.428

### M-283 — A dying mother of two orphans reconciled to death; a hostile priest won over (Paris, before 1925)
- Date: undated
- Status label: sworn at the Church process; recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: A priest of high position (Fr Flamérion SJ, Clamart, died 1925), hostile to devotion, read the Life in one day and was won over. With a charitable lady he cared for a dying poor woman of Paris, condemned by the doctors, who would leave a girl of 16 and a boy of 13 alone in extreme poverty and who despaired and rebelled against Providence for weeks. The priest and the lady each made a novena to Thérèse, unknown to each other. The woman became fully resigned and died with her eyes fixed on a picture of Mary while her two orphans said the rosary.
- The person helped: [EW-135] an unnamed poor woman, Paris [LF-039] a dying woman
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-135] the priest (Flamérion's penitent) wrote a detailed account, sent to Lisieux [LF-039] Fr Flamérion SJ (died 1925), Clamart, reporting to Lisieux (Summarium 1919 p.1032 §2928)
- Status in the sources: [EW-135] sworn testimony [LF-039] recorded by the monastery
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): [EW-135] terminal illness, not named
- Where the sources differ: Names are not given. Flamérion hints at an "extraordinary destiny" of the orphans that he will reveal later. The priest (his penitent) wrote a detailed account sent to Lisieux (Summarium 1919 p. 1032 §2928; PA-20 p. 1103–1104).
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-135] Flamérion hints at an "extraordinary destiny" of the orphans that he will reveal later. [LF-039] Names not given.
- Source entries: M-EW-135, M-LF-039, M-OM-056
- Sources cited: [EW-135] PA-20, p. 1103–1104 [LF-039] LAV p.406–407

### M-284 — Marie Freuchet, La Chevrolière (Nantes), coxalgia cured after a novena

She had suffered much and was completely immobilised; three doctors had tried in vain. During a novena to the Servant of God, Thérèse appeared to the child and told her she would be cured the next day, and she was.

- Witnesses and reporter: the priest (unnamed); Isabelle at the parlour

- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): date not given; no certificate mentioned

## E. Appendix 1: family and earlier events that are not attributed to Thérèse

### M-296 — Mother Elizabeth of St Louis cured by Archbishop d'Aviau, before 1838
- Date: c. 1835
- Status label: recorded by the Carmel or shrine
- What was reported: The first prioress of Lisieux, Sr Elizabeth of St Louis, was threatened early in religious life by a lung disease thought mortal. Archbishop d'Aviau of Bordeaux, visiting his sister at Poitiers, blessed her, asking "Shall we give her the days of Ezechias?" She was completely cured. FND adds that this archbishop later baptised Louis Martin (born at Bordeaux 1823).
- The person helped: Sr Elizabeth of St Louis
- Witnesses and reporter: FND (Carmel foundation book, 1913)
- Status in the sources: recorded by the monastery; the link to Louis's baptism is in F-LF-005
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): "pulmonary disease" (no other detail)
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): No date. FND's claim about d'Aviau baptising Louis: see F-LF-005 (the "archbishop's reported words over the baby").
- Source entries: M-LF-018
- Sources cited: FND p.13

### M-297 — Zélie's inner voice "Fais faire du Point d'Alençon" (Alençon, 8 December 1851)
- Date: 8 Dec 1851
- Status label: family report or tradition
- What was reported: On the feast of the Immaculate Conception (8 Dec 1851), making her bed after a novena, Zélie heard "Have Point d'Alençon made" ("Fais faire du point d'Alençon"); she started the business the next day. Marie says Zélie ascribed it to the Virgin.
- The person helped: [EW-001] Zélie Guérin (Martin)
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-001] Zélie told Marie
- Status in the sources: [EW-001] family tradition
- Where the sources differ: Date: "c. 1851–53" (M-EW-001) and 8 Dec 1851 (M-LF-020). Told by Zélie to Marie decades later (MSC, Cahier).
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): [EW-001] Marie says Zélie ascribed it to the Virgin; told decades later.
- Note: From M-LF-020 (Laveille p. 29–31, 37 fn., 68; Carmel "Le couple Martin"; F-LF-016, F-LF-020). Status in the source: popular tradition / recorded by the family. Medical or natural facts: none. The words in (a) and (b) come from later family tradition, not from a dated note (medium confidence).
- Source entries: M-EW-001, M-LF-020 (partial use: M-LF-020)
- Sources cited: [EW-001] MSC, Cahier

### M-301 — Aunt Sr Marie-Dosithée recovers before Marie's First Communion (Le Mans, c. 1869)
- Date: c. 1869
- Status label: family report or tradition
- What was reported: The aunt was gravely ill; Marie's First Communion was advanced a year so she could see it. Marie, at nine, threw jasmine crowns to St Joseph and was sure of a cure. The aunt recovered, attended, and lived seven more years; dying, she told Marie she owed her seven years of life.
- The person helped: Sr Marie-Dosithée Guérin
- Witnesses and reporter: Marie
- Status in the sources: family memory
- Medical or natural facts (as in the sources): breathing distress ("oppressée"); all hope seemed lost weeks before
- Doubts and other versions (as in the sources): Marie says she lived seven years; she died 1877 — dates roughly consistent.
- Source entries: M-EW-002
- Sources cited: MSC, Cahier

- What was reported: After Hélène's death (1870) Zélie feared she was in Purgatory for a small lie (about a pear she picked up in the garden). Kneeling before the Virgin's statue, she heard a gentle voice: "She is here, beside me" (Laveille: "She is here at my side"). Her tears turned to joy.
- The person helped: [EW-003] Zélie
- Witnesses and reporter: [EW-003] Zélie told Marie
- Status in the sources: [EW-003] family tradition
- Note: From M-LF-020 (Laveille p. 29–31, 37 fn., 68; Carmel "Le couple Martin"; F-LF-016, F-LF-020). Status in the source: popular tradition / recorded by the family. Medical or natural facts: none. The words in (a) and (b) come from later family tradition, not from a dated note (medium confidence).
- Source entries: M-EW-003, M-LF-020 (partial use: M-LF-020)
- Sources cited: [EW-003] MSC, Cahier

- Date: c. 1872
- Status label: family tradition or family letter (Zélie's letters; Laveille)
- What was reported: Before Thérèse's birth Zélie, alone, felt an enormous weight like a wild beast's claw on her shoulder, which she called an attack of the devil (Laveille p. 68, unpublished letter).
